Meet the Vigodas: Conan O’Brien Plays Out His Teenage Dream With L.A. Cover Band

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

When Conan O’Brien takes the stage at the Teragram Ballroom in downtown Los Angeles on Friday night, he’s wearing tight black jeans, a custom black jacket sporting the band’s name, and a wide smile. He greets the crowd by asking how many people had seen his cover band, the Vigodas. A few shout that they had, but the vast majority were there to see him for the first time. And despite his fame, that makes sense. Though he’s probably one of the most recognizable and beloved people in Hollywood, at this point, the band has only done about 10 shows around Southern California since premiering in August.

They draw a wide range of fans. Some know him from his days hosting Late Night, while others seem to be dedicated listeners to his long-running interview podcast, Conan O’Brien Needs a Friend —a smattering appear young enough to have only seen clips of him during his network heyday. The band run through more than a dozen covers — from Elvis Presley and Elvis Costello to Bo Diddley and John Lennon — an eclectic mix of rock & roll spanning the 1950s through the 1980s that is probably best described as the music that won’t get out of O’Brien’s head.

“When I was in college, when everyone else was starting to listen to U2 and Oingo Boingo, I was into hardcore rockabilly,” O’Brien, 63, tells Rolling Stone backstage before the show, running his fingers through the front of his ginger mane, which leaves it in a four-inch wave above his head. “I had the hair for it.” He loved the raucous sounds of Jerry Lee Lewis and early Elvis Presley. “Over the years, I would pick up these songs that were like adrenaline,” he says. “One of them is Ronnie Hawkins’ version of ‘40 Days,’” which they play later during their set.

Though the crowd never seems to break a sweat, they’re engaged through the hour-plus set, occasionally singing along and doing little solo swing dances in place. (There are also a surprising number of couples making out.) But the best part of the night is the overwhelming joy on O’Brien’s face as he jams onstage with four other guys 20 years his junior, whom he only met a few months ago but became fast friends.  Editor’s picks The 250 Greatest Albums of the 21st Century So Far The 100 Best TV Episodes of All Time The 500 Greatest Albums of All Time 100 Best Movies of the 21st Century

O’Brien is probably still best known for his decades as a talk-show host, but it was the podcast that launched the band, he says, when he had singer Norah Jones on in May 2023. “I’ve known Norah over the years, and she always remembered me having a guitar whenever she would come do the late-night show,” O’Brien says. “She said, ‘How’s the guitar going?’ And I said, ‘You know, it’s kind of sad because I don’t really have anyone to play with, and I need someone to kind of kick my ass and help my game.’ She said, ‘I know a guy.’”

The guy was guitarist Jason Roberts, who’d played in Jones’ band for half a decade. He started giving O’Brien lessons, and they would nerd out about their shared love of the Beatles. “We text about the Beatles a lot,” Roberts says later. “We just both love the Beatles so much.”

About six months ago, O’Brien asked Roberts if he’d be down to take their lessons to the next level. “He was just like, ‘Can you help me put a band together?’” Roberts remembers. “And I said, “I know the exact guys for this.” O’Brien tells the same story. “A week later, we’re in Hollywood, and we’re playing,” he says. “I’m just floating, so happy.”

They named themselves after the late actor Abe Vigoda — another towering figure with an expressive face, best known for his roles in The Godfather and Barney Miller. “Abe was a fixture on my old late show, he did hundreds of bits for us, and he was a sweet and hilarious man. I always knew his iconic mug was the face of our band,” says O’Brien. He had never played in a band as a teenager, but always wanted to. “For years and years and years, you could argue that the only reason I wanted a late-night show was so I could have a band,” he says. Back when he was working with the Max Weinberg 7, and later Jimmy Vivino and the Basic Cable Band, he would join them to jam every time they had rehearsal. “I’d put on a guitar and I would play with them and sing songs,” he says. “We had a good repertoire. And then when I went through my Tonight Show madness” — referring to the six months he spent behind the desk of the NBC institution from 2009 to 2010 — “we went out on the road and we did a comedy show, but also it had a lot of music in it too, and that was my happy place.”  Related Content Jack White, Barack Obama, Conan O’Brien Celebrate Stephen Colbert as ‘The Late Show’ Ends Conan O’Brien to Bring His Irreverent Comedy to the Oscars for Third Consecutive Year Jimmy Kimmel Forms Cover Band With Conan O’Brien, Jon Stewart at Netflix Is a Joke Fest Oscars Boss Reveals What You Didn’t See on TV: ‘Sinners’ Performance Ran Long, Speech Snafus, More

O’Brien seems comfortable as a cover man, satisfied to reinterpret the music he loves — often by speeding them up. A few years ago, he ran into Beverly D’Angelo at a party, and she commented to him at just how fast his version of “40 Days” was when she heard it on tour. Earlier this year, when the Rolling Stones asked him to introduce them and their new album at a concert in Brooklyn, he ran into Keith Richards backstage. “Keith said to me, he said, ‘I heard your ‘Monkey Business,’” O’Brien says, referring to the Chuck Berry cover he recorded with Jack White for 2010’s Live at Third Man. O’Brien was surprised he knew who he was, much less had heard his 16-year-old record. “You’re kidding,” he replied. “[Keith] went, ‘You sound like a fucking auctioneer.’” O’Brien laughs. “He’s not wrong.”

Part of the joy of the band is the excuse to tour different venues in Los Angeles. Their first show was at Permanent Records Roadhouse, a tiny club in East L.A. where they played as a surprise guest after Fred Armison’s regularly scheduled set. Since then, they’ve had a tour of Los Angeles’ finest small stages, from the Troubadour to the Lodge Room to the Moroccan Lounge. “I’m a connoisseur of kooky backstages,” he says, remembering a time he hung out with his friend and idol Bob Newhart backstage at the Chicago Theatre. “I had a goofy dressing room, and Bob Newhart said, ‘Conan, no matter what you achieve in show business, whenever you get to the theater and they show you your dressing room, there’s always a piece of dead brown lettuce on the floor. That’s just the way it is.” He smiles at the thought. “It’s very humbling.” Trending Stories A Jesuit Priest Explains How He Facilitated Bob Dylan’s Meeting With the Pope Richard Lloyd, Television's Virtuoso Guitarist, Dead at 75 Houston Mayor Limits Cooperation With ICE: ‘Elections Matter’ Good Riddance to the Cybertruck, the Chariot of the Dumbest Gods

Aside from getting to see the bowels of these Art Deco monuments, the other point of the band, O’Brien says, is to be able to share the stage with other musicians whose work he loves. There are the folks in his outfit, sure — Roberts the guitarist, Erik Kertes on bass, Ben Alleman on keys, and Josh Adams on drums (Rob Jensen filled in on Friday for Adams, who is on tour with the Fruit Bats). But there are all the openers, too, like Malena Cadiz tonight — an Americana singer-songwriter with a lush voice — or Sam Evian, who opened for them at Pappy and Harriet’s out in Joshua Tree a few weeks ago. Each night, he asks those openers to join them for a song or two. “I want it to be a hootenanny,” O’Brien says. “I want it to be something where we roll along and other people jump in. Because I have no future in the music business, but I can be like a small part of it.”

Higher wages don’t have to mean higher inflation. That could matter for Bitcoin

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Preliminary Q3 productivity outpaced hourly compensation, offering a cost counterpoint while inflation keeps the Fed cautious.

US nonfarm businesses produced more per hour in the third quarter, allowing labor costs per unit of output to fall even as hourly compensation rose. For Bitcoin, that offers a potential offset to jobs-driven rate fears: if lower cost pressure helps cool inflation and expected interest rates, financing could become less expensive.

The Bureau of Labor Statistics' preliminary November 5 release showed nonfarm business productivity rising 3.6% as output increased 3.7% and hours worked rose 0.1%. Hourly compensation increased 2.9%, while unit labor costs fell 0.7%. All those quarterly changes are seasonally adjusted annualized rates.

Unit labor costs track compensation relative to output. BLS divides hourly compensation by productivity: when each hour produces more, businesses can pay more per hour without incurring higher labor costs for each unit they produce.

Q3 productivity grew faster than hourly compensation. The quarterly cost decline coexisted with a positive year-over-year cost reading.

Productivity can absorb higher pay; quarterly labor-cost relief does not settle the inflation outlook.

Preliminary Q3 nonfarm business estimates, published November 5, 2026. Quarterly figures are seasonally adjusted annualized rates; the 2.4% figure is year over year. This is not consumer-price inflation, October productivity or a Bitcoin response.

Plot reported Q3 annualized changes on one linear scale: 80 SVG units per percentage point on desktop and 44 on mobile. The 2.4% four-quarter change is shown separately without a comparable bar. No growth-rate subtraction or new calculation.

Preliminary Q3 nonfarm business estimates, published November 5, 2026. Quarterly figures are seasonally adjusted annualized rates; the 2.4% figure is year over year. This is not consumer-price inflation, October productivity or a Bitcoin response.

That complicates a reading of the November 6 employment report as an automatic inflation warning. October payrolls increased 165,000, above the prior 12 months' average monthly gain of 48,000. Average hourly earnings for private nonfarm employees rose 0.4% over the month and 3.1% over a year.

Thanks for signing up to The Catalyst. Look out for your first daily email. If you already receive it, you’re all set.

The reports cover different periods and measures. Third-quarter productivity does not establish how productive workers were in October, and its annualized growth rate cannot simply be subtracted from monthly earnings growth. Higher hiring and pay alone leave the question of labor costs per unit of output unanswered.

Nonfarm business unit labor costs remained 2.4% higher than a year earlier. The quarterly decline eases one source of cost pressure, while leaving both the durability of that improvement and its effect on consumer prices unsettled.

The Fed's latest decision illustrates the policy tension. On October 28, before either November labor release, the Federal Open Market Committee held its target range at 3.75% to 4.00% in a 10-2 vote. Lisa Cook and Beth Hammack preferred a quarter-point increase.

That statement described productivity growth as strong while calling inflation elevated. The two conditions already coexisted in the Fed's assessment, limiting the case for treating better productivity as an automatic signal of easier policy.

For Bitcoin, the financing channel depends on what happens to rate expectations. Expected short-term rates can influence longer-term rates, borrowing terms and the relative attractiveness of assets. A sustained decline in inflation pressure could ease a potential rates headwind for Bitcoin if it brings lower expected rates and funding costs.

Persistent inflation or upward labor-cost revisions would weaken the case for relief in Bitcoin prices.

AMD CEO Lisa Su: 'We are a data center-first company'

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

In a Thursday, November 5 interview with Yahoo Finance (via TechRadar), AMD CEO Lisa Su emphasized the company's AI commitments. Separately, in a Q3 2026 earnings call, Su forecasted an overall "dobule-digit" slump in PC sales in 2027.

"We're a tech company that has a lot of resources and the ability to expand our supply and expand our ability to help our customers. And that's what's led to tremendous growth. So being able to say that my data center business is going to grow 50% sequentially from Q3 to Q4, it's a big step up and it's a testament to how important computing is in the industry today.

"We're looking to double the business⁠—more than double the business⁠—as we go into next year. We are a data center-first company, that is our profile and people need more computing to power all these applications.

[Responding to a question about this past week's slump in AI and tech valuations] "I think the market can sometimes be a little bit short-sighted. Look, it is what it is. What I look at is in the long term, if you believe⁠—which I do, deeply⁠—that computing in many ways equates to intelligence. So the more compute you have, the more intelligent you are, the more intelligent your company is, the more problems you're going to be able to solve, the more you're going to accelerate your business.

"If you believe that foundation, then investing ahead of the curve is the right thing to do because we're always investing for the long term. Long term being, you know, three, four, five years and beyond. And that means you have to make the investments now.

"I've always operated AMD that way. We're always looking at, what do we see over the horizon? And over the horizon, I see AI and super intelligence becoming more and more essential to every aspect of our lives and every aspect of our business. So I believe in this demand and I very much believe that many of the investments that are being made throughout the ecosystem are also seeing some of these same trends."

I found Dr. Su's statements incredibly frustrating, and that all comes down to fundamental differences in perspective and incentive. I do not believe that "computing equates to intelligence" when the output of AI models is so unreliable. They have not changed my life or my work, except to make computer components prohibitively expensive. I do not see "super intelligence" becoming more essential to every aspect of my life in the coming years.

Dr. Su sees the post-US midterm election decline in tech stocks as Mr. Market being short-sighted, but I see overinvestment in data center buildouts and the ludicrous valuation of AI companies as the fundamental error. PC sales will undoubtedly slump next year as the RAMpocalypse continues, but it's hard to swallow tech leaders' sober prognostications on the subject when they caused the shortages and price increases driving the slump.

I believe⁠—and what's more, I hope⁠—that the fever will break and that there will be a correction. Unfortunately, with how much of the economy⁠—particularly 401ks and pension funds⁠—that has been bet on AI's continued growth, I think the coming hangover will be far more democratically distributed than the decision to go on the bender in the first place.

Richard Lloyd, Television’s Fiery and Fluent Guitarist, Dies at 75

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Richard Lloyd, whose fluent and fiery guitar work was a vital element in the influential 1970s-era New York City band Television, died on Saturday at his home in Chattanooga, Tenn., according to the New York Times. The cause of death was bile duct cancer; he was 75.

While commonly associated with the punk rock movement of the era, Television’s sound was anything but punk, driven by Lloyd and frontman Tom Verlaine’s intricate and virtuosic soloing, unusual song structures and Verlaine’s low-key and deadpan vocals. The band’s classic 1977 album “Marquee Moon” is a defining document of the era, including such oft-covered songs as “See No Evil” and the nearly 11-minute title track and casting a vast influence on future alternative rockers like R.E.M. and Sonic Youth. Related Stories Lego Unveils Daft Punk Set Based on 2007 'Alive' Tour, Short Film Collab Set to 'Human After All' Daft Punk and ‘KPop Demon Hunters’ Unite in Lego Holiday Campaign

Yet Television’s peak era was brief, lasting for just two albums before the band split in 1978 and Verlaine and Lloyd pursued solo careers, although the band reformed in 1992 and regrouped several times over the years.

While Verlaine, who died in 2023, is commonly recognized as the band’s frontman and auteur, Lloyd’s contributions were perennially underrated. The pair’s styles were complimentary and in many ways similar, but, broadly speaking, Lloyd brought the fire and Verlaine the finesse. While his struggles with mental health and substance abuse unquestionably marred his career, in addition to his blazing work with Television, Lloyd released nine solo albums and contributed fiery work to Matthew Sweet’s classic 1991 album “Girlfriend,” which also featured fellow CBGB-era guitar master Robert Quine.

Ironically, Television, which formed in 1973, were the band to initiate the Lower Manhattan club CBGB, which proved to be the key incubator for punk rock. According to legend, Verlaine and Lloyd were walking past the venue, saw owner Hilly Kristal outside and asked what kind of music the venue showcased. Told “Country, Bluegrass and Blues,” hence the club’s title, the pair replied that their band played something like that (it did not).

Yet the group secured a booking, other groups like the Ramones, Blondie and Talking Heads soon followed suit, and by 1975 CBGB was the hotbed of a scene and sound that would eventually revolutionize rock music.

Lloyd was born Richard John Alt on Oct. 25, 1951, in Pittsburgh, Lloyd moved with his mother to New York City as an infant. He grew up in Greenwich Village, took up the guitar at an early age and attended concerts at the legendary Fillmore East as a teen; his struggles with mental health and substance abuse also began at this time. He attended New York’s prestigious Stuyvesant High School but dropped out before graduation.

After living briefly in Boston and Los Angeles, he returned to New York and gravitated toward the burgeoning scene around punk forerunners the New York Dolls. Verlaine and his longtime friend Richard Hell had formed a band called the Neon Boys with drummer Billy Ficca, and, after being introduced through Terry Ork (who would later release Television’s first single, “Little Johnny Jewel” on his self named independent label), brought Lloyd into the band. Hell left in 1975 and was replaced by Blondie bassist Fred Smith, raising the band’s instrumental prowess significantly.

Things progressed quickly for the band from there — David Bowie famously called Television “the most original band I’ve seen in New York” — and the band signed with Elektra Records in 1976 and released “Marquee Moon” in February of 1977. Domestic sales were unimpressive but the band, and the scene, captured the imaginations of the influential British rock music press — where punk rock was exploding — and the band actually enjoyed three hit singles, successful tours and two top 10 albums in the U.K.

However, the success was short-lived, as the band — marred by Lloyd’s debilitating heroin addiction — split shortly after the released of its second album, “Adventure.” Despite that, an album titled “Live at the Old Waldorf,” recorded in June of 1978 shortly before the band’s dissolution, finds them at the peak of their powers.

Lloyd regrouped the following year and released his first solo album, “Alchemy,” and played on albums by X’s John Doe as well as Sweet. In 1992, at the height of their alt-rock-era influence, Television reformed, released a self-titled album and toured. They regrouped multiple times over the ensuing years, although usually without Lloyd, whose relationship with Verlaine continued to be fractious. Yet there’s little question that Television and Lloyd had long since made their mark on the history of rock music.

Adam Driver Says No More Kylo Ren: ‘Star Wars’ “Ship Has Sailed”

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

We know Adam Driver has no plans to return to Lena Dunham’s Girls reboot, but what about the Star Wars franchise? Any chance fans will see Kylo Ren again?

“Yeah, yeah, it’s over,” Driver said this week on The Rich Eisen Show podcast with regard to the possibility that he’d return to play Kylo Ren. “No more Star Wars. We tried. We tried to do one more, but they were like, no, he’s dead.”

The character was killed off in The Rise of Skywalker (2019), although Driver says on the podcast that she and Steven Soderbergh had indeed worked on a standalone Kylo Ren movie that never came to fruition. Watch on Deadline

“We got a script together that was really good with Scott Burns and Rebecca Blunt,” Driver tells Eisen about the standalone project that had the working title The Hunt for Ben Solo. “We all worked on it. It was awesome. It was really cool.”

“They didn’t see how Ben Solo was alive,” he said. “And that was that.”

Driver points out that ultimately the decision of whether or not to bring back the character isn’t his to make. “If they wanted to, fine, but it’s not our call,” the actor said. “I think the ship has sailed, though.”

Driver is currently starring in James Gray’s Paper Tiger along with Scarlett Johansson and Miles Teller. Set in 1986 Queens, NY, the film follows two brothers who become entangled in a scheme that ends up involving the Russian mafia. The film premiered at the Cannes Film Festival in May, and is being released November 13 by Neon.

They thought people cared about the Star Wars “universe” rather than the actual characters. No one cares about some random alien planet unless it’s one of the characters we know and love landing there.

Hands-on: I tested a bunch of iPhone Duo cases, these are my favorites [Video]

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

For the first time in a long time, I can say that iPhone cases are fun again! The introduction of the iPhone Duo has brought us a new era of cases in all shapes, sizes, functions, and form factors. We are still in the infancy of this new ecosystem of iPhone Duo-compatible cases, so manufacturers are experimenting with different designs, materials, and features to figure out what works best for Apple’s first foldable. From ultra-thin cases and hinge protection to built-in wallets, kickstands, and origami-style folios. I have tested several new iPhone Duo cases, and these have been my favorite so far.

Be sure to check out our hands-on with all of these cases and more in the video below!

This was actually the very first case that I tried on when I got the iPhone Duo. I thought it looked great, was well-priced at just $29, and had a way to ensure the hinge was covered. Its standout feature is the rotating magnetic kickstand, which allows you to prop up your iPhone Duo in a ton of different orientations. You get:

One thing to note here is that there is zero protection for the outer display. Personally, I was not a fan of the front display being covered by a flap, so I liked that there was nothing on the front of the iPhone Duo. Lastly, this is one of the few cases that does not rely on adhesive strips to stay on the Duo.

You can get the Baseus iPhone Duo case on Amazon for $29.

This one has been my go-to since the dust has settled. I’m someone who has always enjoyed having the thinnest cases possible that still offer some rigidity and protection. That’s exactly what this Aulumu case offers for the iPhone Duo. You get:

As I said, this has been my favorite because of its thin, lightweight nature. It also uses adhesive on the front of the iPhone Duo to ensure it doesn’t fall off. The good news is that it leaves no residue. But it does not have any additional features, such as a built-in stand or wallet, nor does it have any hinge protection. So that is something to consider.

The Aulumu ADOU is available in two colors: Black and Blue. You can get it from Aulumu’s website for $89.

The Smartish Wallet case takes a completely different approach by combining a protective case with a built-in wallet. If you have noticed, while the iPhone Duo does have MagSafe, many current MagSafe accessories do not fit it. They also have a portion of it sticking out of the bottom; this includes MagSafe wallets. So Smartish built the wallet directly into the case, and it’s genius. You get:

So this case covers every aspect of the iPhone Duo while remaining relatively thin and allowing you to carry up to 4 cards. But because there is a built-in wallet, you lose access to the MagSafe portion of the iPhone, so you cannot wirelessly charge the iPhone Duo when the case is on. If you are ok with that trade-off, then you will love this case.

The Smartish Wallet Slayer case comes in five different colors and designs. You can get these cases on Amazon starting at $39.

If you are looking for something a little more rugged, the UAG Mouve case is worth looking into. UAG has always focused on protective cases, and this one brings the same philosophy and design language while adding an adjustable built-in kickstand. You get:

What impressed me the most was the stability of the kickstand, especially compared to some other cases I’ve tested. Since the kickstand has a hexagonal design, it has flat sides. This is what gives it the additional stability. Combine that with protective corners, raised camera and screen edges, and a premium feel, and you have a great option for people who want that extra protection.

You can get the UAG Mouve case from Amazon for $84. It comes in two colors: White and Ash(grey).

MOFT is one of my favorite and most used brands. They are known for creating clever, origami-inspired accessories, so they decided to bring that to the iPhone Duo. They created a magnetic folding cover that doubles as a folding, versatile stand with multiple viewing angles. This is the most different of the bunch; you get:

I really like the simplicty and creativity behind this one. It is just one panel that slaps onto the iPhone via magnets. One thing to note about this is that there is no rail protection. This is very similar to Apple’s own folio case for iPhone. With the MOFT case, while the rails are exposed, there is also a raised lip to it, so if you do place it on a surface rail side down, it technically will not touch because of the raised lip of the MOFT folio. So if you are ok with that slight compromise and you like the simplicity of this case, then I recommend checking it out.

You can pre order this case on Amazon for $69 and deliveries start November 27th. It comes in two color matching variats: white and Navy blue.

These cases are all so new that manufacturers can have fun again seeing what works with the iPhone Duo form factor. Consumers can also see what they like, what they dont like, what can be improved. This is just the beginning and I am so excited to see where these cases end up evolving. But there's good news: it won't! As the action RPG now has a 1.0 release date locked in, alongside a look at what you can expect when it arrives.

As shared by developer Grinding Gear Games, Path of Exile 2 will be launching into 1.0 next month on December 11th. Probably the biggest aspect of the update is the fact that the game's campaign will be completely playable, with the fifth and sixth acts being added in wrapping up the story that's been built up over its early access release. The first is the Shadow, which appropriately introduces a stealth mechanic. As you might expect of such a class, while you're in stealth, "monsters can't see you until you get within a short distance of them." Being in said mode also bolsters your dagger with a range of bonuses, and the Shadow also has another weapon type available to them, traps. You prepare these in stealth, "then trigger them automatically as you strike. Traps explode in the order they were thrown, allowing you to set up the perfect combo!" And the other class is the Duelist, who has the new stance mechanic. This lets you strengthen your sword skills, "enhancing each of them in different ways," with various stances allowing for "even deadlier combinations."

Beyond that, there's a new league to try out that lets you team up with Varek and his mercs to "conquer Wraeclast!" A whole bunch of new items have been added in, with many being revamped, and you'll also find new skills gems and support gems reworked. The passive skill tree has also seen a makeover, and a number of classes have also received some improvements. You can read about all of that in more detail right here.

After a six-year hiatus, American Airlines’ most exclusive lounge offering at Los Angeles International Airport (LAX) is back in business.

This week, the carrier reopened Flagship Dining at LAX. That’s American’s a la carte restaurant-style experience, which is available to customers flying in its most premium seats.

It had been closed since 2020 but is now open again after a face-lift.

And it also has a new access policy at LAX — and at the two other American hubs that offer the service.

American now offers Flagship Dining at three of its hubs:

At those airports, you'll find the a la carte dining experience is available within its Flagship Lounge, AA's premium lounge that's open to premium cabin passengers and AAdvantage elites taking long-haul flights.

Instead of grabbing food at the buffet, Flagship Dining offers flyers a sit-down meal with table service — we’re talking a full menu with coursed-out dishes, all complimentary.

In the past, the airline limited access to Flagship Dining to passengers flying in its Flagship First cabin on the Boeing 777-300ER or Airbus A321T aircraft.

But as the carrier gets set to phase out its international-style first-class cabin, access to Flagship Dining is opening up to more passengers.

In September, American began allowing passengers in its Flagship business-class cabins to sit for its exclusive dining experience.

If you’re flying out of DFW, MIA or LAX, you can have a Flagship Dining meal if you are:

That change is especially notable at LAX, because it allows business-class passengers to use Flagship Dining on certain transcontinental flights. To get in, you'll need to have a ticket that's marketed as "Flagship" (domestic first-class tickets won't be enough).

Like with any restaurant or airport lounge, space is limited. You can join the waitlist for a table after arriving at the Flagship Lounge in LA, Dallas and Miami.

American used to offer Flagship Dining at John F. Kennedy International Airport (JFK). Today, the carrier instead operates its three premium lounges (Greenwich, Chelsea and Soho) in Terminal 8 with partner British Airways.

Meanwhile, its newest Flagship Lounge at Philadelphia International Airport (PHL) features a la carte dining within the main club.

Speaking of American’s “Flagship” product, the end is rapidly approaching for Flagship First.

Nov. 19 will mark the last date AA offers long-haul first-class as a stand-alone product on its 777s.

Those jets are getting refreshed with new interiors, with the airline ditching first-class and adding the highest business-class seat count it's ever offered.

American will phase out Flagship First on transcontinental service in March.

I Wouldn’t Dream of Playing the Zelda: Ocarina of Time Remake Right Now

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Some things are sacred. No, not like that!

Unlike you, probably, I am not playing Nintendo's Switch 2 remake of The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time right now. I even turned down a kind offer from my editor to review it. This despite the fact that it might be my favorite Zelda game (mostly by virtue of being the first one I played) and indubitably one of the greatest games ever — as close to a Citizen Kane as the medium has.

I've read the ecstatic reviews, several of them by friends of mine, like Keza MacDonald and Christian Donlan, and of course Polygon's own Giovanni Colantonio, who I know to be people of good sense and great taste who share my reverence for this game and this series. I trust them.

Nintendo’s excellent Switch 2 remake recaptures the soul of a masterpiece

I also trust Nintendo, especially now it's clear the game was developed by the in-house Zelda team and not farmed out to an external studio. I trust producers Eiji Aonuma and Hidemaro Fujibayashi, veterans of Zelda's golden age and architects of its great Breath of the Wild renaissance, who Gio talked to this week. I trust their decision to pass Ocarina on to younger developers at Nintendo to train and inspire them, but also to inject the aging game with youth and life.

Nintendo's top Legend of Zelda producers talk the remake, the future, and more

I also know that Ocarina needs the work. I played a little of the Nintendo 64 original recently, and while I remained entranced by its strangely haunted vibe, I found that, like so many early 3D games, it is fighting a battle with clunky, barely-ready technology that it has been slowly losing over the last 28 years. I'm honestly not sure that it's still fun to play now.

Zelda's most infamous dungeon is no longer a frustrating rite of passage

And this matters to me, because my 10-year-old son loves Zelda — which, to him, means Tears of the Kingdom. He thinks Tears is the best game ever, and I wouldn't necessarily disagree. He's read the Ocarina of Time manga and fiddled about with other games in the series, including the Link's Awakening remake and The Wind Waker, but never settled into any of them. Honestly, I warned him off trying to play the original Ocarina.

So the Ocarina remake arrives at a perfect time for him. He is beyond excited about it. He will get it, and his own Switch 2 to play it on, for Christmas. Put yourself in his shoes. (Maybe you once were, and got the original Ocarina, or the 3DS version, as a childhood Christmas gift yourself.) Can you imagine anything better?

I just know that if I played the game before him, even surreptitiously, I would be stealing the magic of that moment. As desperate as I am to see the work of Aonuma and Fujibayashi's team, I can't countenance it. So I'll wait with him.

Also… If I'm honest, maybe I'm not just doing it for him. Because I'll be unwrapping my own copy on Christmas morning. I'll be playing this magical game away from work, out of step with the discourse, in the cozy, contented, quiet bubble of the holidays, surrounded by my family. Maybe I'll feel like I'm 10, too. Like I said: can you imagine anything better?

ZA/UM, the makers of Disco Elysium — well, some of them; it's complicated — return with another wonderfully funny, dark, and emotionally complex narrative RPG that uses tabletop dice rolls to enumerate the failings of human beings. One in particular: a not good, very bad spy who is trying to atone for a terrible betrayal.

Disco Elysium follow-up Zero Parades has serious staying power

This 1988 NES classic from Capcom is simply one of the all-time great action platformers. It's all the more remarkable since the developers basically had to cobble it together in their spare time after the original was deemed a sales disappointment. Now Mega Man is a treasured franchise, and due for a return next year.

I'm not playing Ocarina, but I am allowed to listen to the eight-track soundtrack sample that just dropped on Nintendo Music, and it's absolutely delicious. The exquisitely produced and performed new arrangements of Koji Kondo's immortal tunes are both a massive upgrade and resounding proof that Kondo had loaded his music with all this drama, humor, and sophistication from the start. Impossible to pick a favorite from the full flamenco of "Gerudo Valley," or the squawking penny whistles, accordions, oboes, and fiddles of "Market," or "Kakariko Village"'s plangeant harmonica. But let's go with the 10-minute "Hyrule Field Main Theme" for sheer epic sweep.

It’s from the Deku Trio, because of course it is

Celtic star's telepathic connection with strike partner and why his best is yet to come

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Callum Osmand continues to highlight his considerable potential by making his mark at Celtic, but the young forward is adamant that what supporters have witnessed so far is only the beginning.

The 20-year-old has experienced a whirlwind introduction to first-team football in Glasgow, with the demands of competing for a place at one of Scotland's biggest clubs providing a steep learning curve. Yet his decisive contribution to Celtic's dramatic 3-2 Europa League victory over Besiktas offered another glimpse of the qualities that have made him such an intriguing prospect.

Did you know with a Digital Subscription to The Scotsman, you can get unlimited access to the website including our premium content, as well as benefiting from fewer ads, loyalty rewards and much more.

Introduced by Martin O’Neill in the second half, Osmand wasted little time making his presence felt against the Turkish side, scoring just 43 seconds after coming on before delivering a perfectly weighted pass for Camilo Duran to complete his double. His impact transformed the contest and secured Celtic a much-needed victory.

“Yeah, it was crazy. It's a surreal feeling when it's my first touch as well,” he reflected. “I was a bit nervous that I'd shanked it, but no, I'm happy that I caught it nicely. He (O’Neill) knows that I know what he expects from me, so it was about making sure I can come and bring some energy.”

His burgeoning partnership with Duran was another highlight. Their understanding was evident in the build-up to Celtic's vital third goal, with the former Fulham forward displaying excellent vision to pick out the Colombian's run in behind.

“I thought he was offside, to be honest!” Osmand admitted. “I was looking at the bench saying 'Close?', and they were like, 'Yeah, it's tight', but I know where he's going. He's such a sharp striker, so I know if I put that in the space, the keeper is not getting anywhere near it. He's a top, top player, so I can't fault him at all.”

Their connection is extending beyond the pitch, too. Despite a language barrier, Osmand feels the pair have struck up a genuine friendship.

“His English is really not great, but somehow we manage to talk to each other a lot,” he laughed. “I think I find him quite a good friend of mine now. Although we might not speak many words, you can tell just by looking. We talk to each other a lot. Maybe it's little words, but we get on really well.

“Camilo is unbelievable, a really top player. You can see even by the way he walks and the way he moves. I learn off him every day. He's a great guy.”

The victory over Besiktas could prove pivotal to Celtic's European ambitions, and Osmand hopes it will provide the confidence required to approach the remaining fixtures with greater freedom.

“For us, it felt like a must-win. That was our mindset going into the game and it’s a huge result,” he explained. “We knew we could still qualify, but we knew we had to get something out of Thursday night, and I think the boys done amazing.

“Sometimes when you're not as confident, it's tough to play the football you want to play, but as you can see, when we're confident, it just goes forward every time. We know when we're not playing as well, and we'll be the first, as players, to critique each other and critique ourselves before anyone else. It is about just staying together, building and getting a result like this to take into the next game.”

Osmand is equally candid about his own development, insisting there is still plenty of room for improvement despite his encouraging start. He pointed out what will be key to taking his game to the next level.

“I'm nowhere near where I want to be personally, and there's stuff I need to tidy up on, like decision-making and all this stuff,” Osmand admitted. “But I feel like the more games I play, the more I understand and the more I learn. So on a personal note, that's what I want to do, just keep learning and keep understanding my role and obviously the players you're playing with.

“I've still only played a handful of games, so it's tough learning, but I feel like when you get thrown in the deep end, it's the best way to learn. It's just as long as I can get better every game, that's all I really want to focus on.”

His recent deployment on the left wing has also offered another opportunity to broaden his game. Although not a position he has traditionally occupied, Osmand is relishing the licence to express himself.

“I’m really enjoying it. It's a really fun position and it's very, very attacking,” he commented. “I have a lot of freedom and the staff give me that freedom and tell me to go and try and make something happen. It's a great feeling when you know you can just go and impose your game.”

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Researchers found extreme levels of mercury in sediment cores in Mexico ‪—‬ and it’s all because the Maya loved red paint.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

The Maya used so much red paint that they accidentally poisoned their lakes and waterways with mercury, a new study finds.

The problem was cinnabar, a vivid-red mercury-sulfide mineral that can be used as the pigment vermilion in red paint. The run-off from the paint can enter waterways, where anaerobic bacteria then transform cinnabar into highly toxic methylmercury in the sediments of lakes. Over time, the methylmercury accumulates to higher levels in the food chain.

The ancient Maya, a people who lived in what is now southern Mexico and Central America and whose descendants are still around today, painted almost everything red. Archaeologists have found countless artifacts that the Maya reddened, including sculptures, monuments, walls, plazas, pottery and the dead for ceremonial funerals. Cinnabar was a common pigment in the paint they made, along with red ocher, which gets its color from a nontoxic iron mineral called hematite.

But the Maya passion for the color red — which to them represented the sun, blood and life — caused an environmental disaster when the nearby waters were poisoned by the cinnabar runoff, said study first author Matthew Waters, a paleolimnologist (a scientist who studies ancient waterways) at Auburn University in Alabama.

"They would have seen a strange color change in the water … and a drop in its productivity, such as fish for protein," he told Live Science. The Maya depended heavily on freshwater fisheries, including for freshwater shellfish.

Previous studies had already found evidence for high levels of mercury in the Maya's southern highlands and south-central lowlands, but no one had fully examined levels of the toxic mineral in Mexico's Yucatán Peninsula.

To investigate, Waters and his colleagues studied mercury levels in Maya-era mud in the Yucatán, and found overwhelming signs of mercury poisoning, probably from cinnabar paint, they reported in their study, published Nov. 4 in the journal Science Advances.

Get the world’s most fascinating discoveries delivered straight to your inbox.

"We did not suspect [the Maya] used it in the quantities that we were seeing," he said.

The study found extreme levels of mercury in sediment cores from two Maya-era sites: Cobá Lake, a shallow lake beside the Maya city of that name, and Cenote Xcach (also spelled X-Canché), a flooded sinkhole beside a ruined city in the jungle that few have excavated.

The sediment cores chronicle the composition of earth and water over more than 2,000 years, and the times of mercury poisoning coincide with several periods during the Preclassic and Classic periods of the Maya civilization from about A.D. 40 to 820.

Waters explained that the Environmental Protection Agency in the U.S. has determined it is unsafe to drink water with mercury levels above 2 parts per billion, while the official limit for the level of mercury in fish, where it builds up over time, is 1 part per million — any number above that means the fish can be seized by authorities.

Severe poisoning occurs when the mercury levels in human blood exceed about 100 parts per billion, from drinking contaminated water or eating contaminated marine animals, such as fish. In humans, mercury poisoning can cause severe symptoms, including permanent lung damage, brain damage and death.

The reddish hue of the Maya sun god Kinich Ahau, made between the sixth and ninth centuries, now housed in Mexico’s National Museum of Anthropology, shows the Maya's love for mercury.

The most polluted parts of the Yucatan cores had a mercury level of 2.3 parts per million, the team discovered. "They experienced this for decades and, in one case, for centuries," Waters said.

The researchers then examined the sediment cores for signs of environmental damage caused by the mercury poisoning. They found that the cores lacked tiny fossils of freshwater shellfish, indicating that the high mercury levels were killing marine life. The team also noticed that the sediment cores had hardly any traces of algal blooms, which should have happened frequently, Waters said. That's likely because algae could not survive in the poisoned water nutrients.

Scholars debate why the Maya civilization collapsed in the eighth and ninth centuries, but a leading suspect is the disruption of food supplies caused by drought.

But although mercury levels were high during the Preclassic and Classic periods, the environmental damage caused by mercury levels had been falling in many places for hundreds of years by the time of the Maya collapse, Waters said, although it may have been a contributing factor.

Cinnabar does not occur naturally in the Yucatán, so it must have been imported there, probably from what's now Honduras, the researchers said. It seems these cinnabar imports eventually stopped at Cobá Lake, and the poisoning there fell to a background level of about 20 parts per billion by A.D. 520. However, the sediment cores showed that the mercury poisoning continued at Cenote Xcach until the collapse, they said.

Waters, M. N., Wooten, S. L., Abrook, A. M., Brenner, M., Curtis, J. H., Langdon, P. G., Medina-Gonzalez, R. M., Paradeisis-Stathis, S., & Vachula, R. S. (2026). Toxic mercury, aquatic ecosystem collapse, and the ancient Lowland Maya. Science Advances, 12(45), eaeh3280. https://doi.org/10.1126/sciadv.aeh3280

Tom writes mainly about science, space, archaeology, the Earth and the oceans. He has also written for the BBC, NBC News, National Geographic, Scientific American, Air & Space, and many others.

I'm kinda just playing chiller PvE, here.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

I've been stuck into World of Warcraft: Forever—at the time of writing, I'm around 15 hours /played (and will likely be 20+ hours by the time you're reading this) on my warrior. I chose the roleplay ruleset because, well, I'm a roleplayer. I've been writing and being cringe in videogames for over 15 years.

RP in WoW is, actually, what got me into writing in the first place. My clear and obvious mastery over the written word was hard-won by being a terribly annoying edgelord death knight, for which I apologise.

I was curious about the implementation of the roleplay ruleset back when it was properly announced—back then, I thought it was great that we're being given our own place, but being completely split off from "normal" ruleset (which I'll be calling PvE for simplicity) didn't seem necessary to me.

Having played in the roleplay ruleset for a little while, my opinion has only been vindicated. Splitting PvE and PvP rulesets does make sense. For instance, a resource node might be easier to gather on PvE, throwing the economic balance of the PvP server out of the window. But beyond technical limitations, a complete, strict divide between RP and PvE just has me scratching my head.

There are things I like about having our own little nook of the Forever servers to putz about on. For starters, the vibes are a lot chiller. I don't want to cast generalisations about players, but I do think roleplayers are just smarter, sexier, have bigger muscles, and will solve climate change.

In seriousness, I do think thinking about your character's place in the world and having a bent towards immersion does make you a chiller person in general. I've experienced vanishingly little toxicity or hostility. The absolute worst instance was a tank getting a little passive-aggressive about mining nodes in my Deadmines run. That's it.

But what I foresaw is bearing out: Nobody is really roleplaying in instanced or group content, and that's because we legitimately never have done so. Most RP takes place either spontaneously out in the open world, or in dedicated hot spots. Even in the most diehard MMO communities, people aren't talking in ye olde English while pushing Vancleef's face in.

Keep up to date with the most important stories and the best deals, as picked by the PC Gamer team.

There are some people who are always in-character, sure, but that portion of the playerbase is incredibly rare. We're talking a sliver of an already-small population of a videogame, here.

As for the strict rules on naming conventions—yeah, I'm seeing a lot more lore-accurate names, but I'm also seeing plenty of joke ones. And if I'm being perfectly honest, these gag names really haven't ever bothered me; people being disruptive or annoying when I'm roleplaying are a massive pain, but if Tsundere Cowbell the paladin wants to watch me write, my immersion isn't shattered.

Overall, I think keeping us on our own server architecture for the most part is the smarter plan, especially given layering would dilute roleplayers so much that RP would be hard to find. However, I don't think there's any real reason to disallow us from jumping to the Normal ruleset servers when we group up with someone from there—or giving us an option to travel between them of our own accord.

Just as we tend to swap between in-character for roleplay and out-of-character for, y'know, playing the videogame—I think most of us would be happy to have the choice as to whether we want to futz about on the roleplay layer or go hang out with our non-theatre kid friends on the PvE ruleset.

Especially given, as time goes on, population might become an issue. Believe it or not, roleplayers still do like to raid and gear up, and if they should be able to migrate on characters they've invested literal dozens of hours into, rather than having to start all over again.

Best MMOs: Most massiveBest strategy games: Number crunchingBest open world games: Unlimited explorationBest survival games: Live craft loveBest horror games: Fight or flight

Harvey's history with games started when he first begged his parents for a World of Warcraft subscription aged 12, though he's since been cursed with Final Fantasy 14-brain and a huge crush on G'raha Tia. He made his start as a freelancer, writing for websites like Techradar, The Escapist, Dicebreaker, The Gamer, Into the Spine—and of course, PC Gamer. He'll sink his teeth into anything that looks interesting, though he has a soft spot for RPGs, soulslikes, roguelikes, deckbuilders, MMOs, and weird indie titles. He also plays a shelf load of TTRPGs in his offline time. Don't ask him what his favourite system is, he has too many.

SABO Project "softens divisions" inside Manhattan apartment

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Photography by Alexandre Delaunay

New York and Paris design studio SABO Project has introduced rounded partitions and bold green accents to a compact apartment on Manhattan’s Lower East Side.

The 728-square-foot (68-square-metre) residence was renovated for a family of three with the help of contractor Alexar Interiors.

SABO Project opened up the plan and rounded the corners of walls and partitions between the various functional areas to visually improve the flow.

“The design aims to soften divisions between spaces so the apartment is experienced as a central living room extending into surrounding alcoves,” said the studio.

The apartment carries an industrial aesthetic thanks to exposed breeze-block walls, metal ductwork, concrete ceiling and polished concrete flooring.

Steel structural beams are painted a shade of mid green that is also applied to the lower kitchen cabinets, and appears on curtains and in artwork around the home.

“The apartment balances the rough materiality of exposed masonry blocks and ductwork with the soft textures of bespoke curtains, shades, and rugs,” said SABO Project. “Bold graphic forms animate curtains designed for each room.”

In the open-plan living area, a circular rug in the centre is mirrored on the ceiling by a white-painted circle that’s crossed with a minimalist light fixture.

Along one wall, stacks of concrete blocks with arch-shaped openings form a bench and a display console, accompanied at one end by a small wooden table.

“A long bench runs from the main room into a small outdoor space, conceived as an interior and enclosed with light-filtering fabric and a delicate steel mesh,” said the studio.

This small sun room is accessed via sliding glass doors from the living area and is also visible from the kitchen through a fixed glazed panel.

Here, green outdoor furniture from the Bouroullec brothers’ Palissade collection offers additional seating alongside the breeze-block bench.

Two identically sized bedrooms – one for the parents and another for their child – are decorated with the same colour and material palette as the rest of the apartment.

Quartets of breeze blocks are stacked on their sides to form nightstands with cubbies, and green appears again in the bedding.

In the bathroom, white tiles with green grout get progressively smaller as they reach the ceiling, creating a subtle gradient effect through density.

A lozenge-shaped mirror and sconce, and a basin with filleted corners continue the rounded forms found throughout the residence.

SABO Project was founded by architect Alexandre Delaunay, who works between New York and Paris on a range of residential, commercial and cultural projects.

The studio’s portfolio includes a Brooklyn loft featuring an irregular clustered storage unit, a Parisian apartment with multicoloured flooring and space-saving stairs, and another residence in the French capital that includes peg-hole walls and a curved child-proof staircase.

Greenwashing Won’t Save You — How Gen Z Is Rewriting the Rules of Brand Authenticity

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

You're viewing the US website. Looking for the Site edition?

Discover how Gen Z is redefining brand authenticity, challenging greenwashing and demanding transparency, sustainability and real action.

Opinions expressed by Entrepreneur contributors are their own.

Walk into any corner store or scroll past a sponsored post, and you see it right away. Everything is suddenly “eco-friendly,” “sustainably sourced” or “carbon-neutral.” Companies slap a leaf icon on a plastic wrapper, paint the box olive green and call it a day.

They think this trick still works. It doesn’t.

If your marketing plan leans on empty claims to win over younger buyers, you’re playing a risky game. Greenwashing won’t save your startup. It might actually sink it faster than bad cash flow. Gen Z is rewriting the rules of brand honesty, and they have zero patience for corporate spin.

For two decades, big brands got away with soft marketing. Slap a vague promise about 2050 goals on a billboard, hire a PR agency to write a press release about planting trees and watch sales climb.

Gen Z grew up with a phone in their hand and a search bar at their fingertips. They don’t read your ad copy and take it at face value. They check your supply chain. They search your brand name on TikTok to see if employees are calling out bad workplace habits. They can even look up your parent company’s tax filings.

When a brand gets caught lying about its footprint, the backlash is fast, loud and it doesn’t go away. A fake green label doesn’t make you look responsible. It makes you look dishonest.

Young shoppers don’t expect your startup to be perfect. They know running a business takes resources and that shipping physical goods creates carbon output. What actually bothers them is the theater.

Take a small digital-first startup that sells modular workspace gear. Instead of hiding behind vague terms like “100% Earth-Friendly,” they list the exact components: 80% recycled aluminum, 20% new ABS plastic because of structural limits, made in a factory that runs on a grid with a high coal mix.

That kind of honesty instantly sets them apart from competitors shouting about saving the planet.

Gen Z wants hard facts over soft poetry. They’d rather buy from a business that admits where it falls short than one that pretends to have no flaws at all.

To win customer trust today, startups need to rethink how they talk to people. Here’s how honest brands are changing the story:

Show the receipts: Don’t just say your sourcing is ethical. Prove it. If you run a fast-casual food stall, put the actual invoices from local farm suppliers near your register or post them online every week.

Name the trade-offs: If recycled paper packaging doubles your unit cost and pushes up your retail price, just say so. Customers respect the honesty.

Stop waiting to be perfect: You don’t need a flawless environmental record to speak up. Share your current numbers today, set short-term goals for next month and report back when you fall short.

Modern shoppers know every purchase involves some kind of trade-off. When you pretend your product has zero impact, you insult their intelligence.

Take a modern specialty tea brand as an example. Instead of claiming their tea bags are fully biodegradable, when most actually need industrial composting facilities that regular city systems don’t have, they put a warning label right on the front of the pouch:

“These tea bags need commercial high-heat composting. If you toss them in your garden, they’ll take years to break down. Here’s how to dispose of them the right way.”

That one blunt line builds more brand trust than a million-dollar ad campaign built on fluffy green claims. It shows respect for the buyer’s intelligence.

If you want your business to survive this shift, you need to drop the corporate buzzwords and rebuild how you talk to people, starting now.

Cut words like “green,” “pure,” “natural” and “conscious” from your site unless you back them up with a specific third-party certification or a real number. If a claim can’t be checked, delete it.

Use your social channels to show operational hiccups. If a supplier fails to meet your material standards, post about the delay. Explain why you turned down the shipment. Turn your operational struggles into proof of your standards.

Big-budget, super-polished video ads read as dishonest to an audience raised on user-generated content. A quick, unedited founder video shot on a phone explaining a packaging decision lands far deeper than a slick commercial.

Gen Z isn’t asking every business to solve global problems on its own. They’re asking businesses to stop using those problems as a sales tactic.

If your product is good, let it stand on its own. If your operations are still a work in progress, show the process. Drop the green paint, publish your real numbers and talk to your customers like actual people.

Trust is not something you buy with a clever campaign anymore. It’s something you earn by showing up, dropping the spin and telling the whole truth every single time.

Walk into any corner store or scroll past a sponsored post, and you see it right away. Everything is suddenly “eco-friendly,” “sustainably sourced” or “carbon-neutral.” Companies slap a leaf icon on a plastic wrapper, paint the box olive green and call it a day.

They think this trick still works. It doesn’t.

If your marketing plan leans on empty claims to win over younger buyers, you’re playing a risky game. Greenwashing won’t save your startup. It might actually sink it faster than bad cash flow. Gen Z is rewriting the rules of brand honesty, and they have zero patience for corporate spin.

Solana’s October Upgrade Review Puts 200ms Slots and $5B in Tokenized Assets in Focus

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Strict editorial policy that focuses on accuracy, relevance, and impartiality

Morbi pretium leo et nisl aliquam mollis. Quisque arcu lorem, ultricies quis pellentesque nec, ullamcorper eu odio.

TL;DR: Solana Foundation’s November 6 ecosystem review says two protocol changes went live in October: a reduction in target slot time to 200 milliseconds and Alpenglow consensus activation. It also reports a record $5 billion in real-world asset value. These are different metrics, and none alone proves a better experience for every application.

After a month filled with announcements, Solana has published a consolidated account of what changed on its network. The Foundation’s October 2026 ecosystem roundup, released November 6, links a pair of major performance upgrades with increased tokenized-asset activity and a growing roster of payment integrations.

The review is useful because it separates infrastructure that has activated from applications and partnerships still seeking wider use. It also offers a chance to examine how technical speed claims translate into financial operations.

According to the Foundation, a final stage of SIMD-0525 activated on October 9, reducing target slot times from the original 400 milliseconds to 200 milliseconds. Shorter slots aim to reduce the interval at which network leaders produce blocks. They do not guarantee that every transaction is confirmed within 200 milliseconds under all network conditions.

A second change followed on October 21 when Alpenglow replaced TowerBFT consensus on mainnet. The protocol targets roughly 150-millisecond finality. Finality describes when a transaction is sufficiently settled according to the network’s rules, and should not be confused with transaction submission, application response time or a merchant receiving funds in a bank account.

NewsBTC covered the Alpenglow activation earlier in the autumn. The new roundup does not make it a second launch. It provides a broader, dated view of how the two changes fit together, while reminding readers that production reliability has to be measured over time.

The Foundation reports that real-world assets on Solana reached $5 billion in value during October, alongside approximately $60 billion in decentralized-exchange trading volume and 14 million addresses holding stablecoins. These numbers measure different things. Asset value is not trading volume; a blockchain address is not necessarily a person; and gross turnover is not revenue to applications or validators.

The review includes new or expanded connections to traditional finance. J.P. Morgan Asset Management brought tokenized money market funds to the network through Morgan Money. The Foundation also describes stock-related products from Securitize and investment portfolios from Ondo, subject to their own eligibility conditions. For institutional users, the question is not only whether a token can move quickly but whether its legal ownership, redemption rights and custody arrangements remain clear.

That is also the theme behind Solana’s delivery-versus-payment programme, which seeks to coordinate an asset and its payment in one settlement process. Better throughput can help, but it cannot replace legal and operational safeguards.

The Foundation’s update lists stablecoin and remittance activity involving Samsung Wallet, Fiserv and Western Union, as well as integrations with business-payment services. Some products have launched, while other announcements refer to planned support, geographically limited access or commercial pilots. Those distinctions should survive any headline about ‘mainstream adoption’.

For developers and validators, the next meaningful evidence is sustained uptime, consistent finality under load, integration reliability and the cost of operating infrastructure. For investors, it is whether tokenized value corresponds to assets customers can actually redeem and use. Solana’s October was busy by the Foundation’s account; November’s challenge is showing the upgrades make routine financial activity more dependable, not merely more impressive on paper.

Why Project Hail Mary Author Andy Weir Thinks Sci-Fi Has 'Grown Up' Recently [Exclusive]

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

In an interview with /Film's Jeremy Mathai, Andy Weir, the author of the hit novels "The Martian" and "Project Hail Mary," recognized that the world has never been more accepting of sci-fi. What was once relegated to the realm of "nerds" and outsiders several generations ago is now embraced by everyone. And that, Weir noted, has allowed artists to tell more serious and dramatic sci-fi stories. When a mass audience takes it seriously, the genre, consequently, grows up.

And Weir has been doing his part to raise the genre's profile. The films based on his novels — Ridley Scott's "The Martian," and Phil Lord and Christopher Miller's "Project Hail Mary," to call them out by name — are known for their scientific rigor (especially "The Martian"). And although possessed of fantastical sci-fi conceits, both movies pay a lot of attention to science, research, and the power of human intelligence. These are movies for grown-ups that don't soft-pedal or water down the details of astronomy, physics, language, or botany. As such, these films come across as being comparatively "adult."

Weir also credited this in part to the various superhero films that've come out in the 21st Century. Many of those movies have sci-fi elements, and even a piece of Marvel fantasy fluff can bring more grown-up credibility to the genre. As Weir put it, "The superhero movie phenomenon helped bring more science fiction elements into the mainstream."

To be sure, artificial intelligence, parallel universes, and time travel frequently show up in superhero stories, and the genre's popularity has made a lot of those strange scientific theories a regular part of mainstream thinking. Yes, in that sense, even the box office smash-hit that was "Iron Man" helped the sci-fi genre grow up.

The recent boom of high-quality sci-fi television also came up during this discussion with Andy Weir. Just turn on Apple TV alone, and you'll find the cream of the crop in terms of modern sci-fi TV, be it "Severance," "Pluribus," "Foundation," or "Silo." Commenting on this, Weir noted:

"I think [the genre has] grown up a bit. So, it used to be science fiction was the bailiwick of just nerds. In the '80s, there were a few sci-fi shows. There was 'Star Trek: Next Generation' toward the end of the '80s. Then there was things like 'Quantum Leap,' and stuff like that. But other than that, the sci-fi shows that did exist were kind of cheesy. You got 'Battlestar Galactica' [...] It wasn't big mainstream entertainment."

Some "Battlestar Galactica" and "Quantum Leap" fans might bristle at these comments, but Weir is right on one point: sci-fi shows certainly seem more common nowadays. That, he argued, along with superhero movies and people now having access to more high-tech items, have further expanded their tolerance and devotion to the genre. As he put it:

[N]ow we've had a whole generation of people grow up in the computer age [...] [P]eople are a lot more socially accepting of science fiction as a legitimate setting [...] I love that there are serious, very well written, innovative and unique science fiction shows out there now."

Basically, sci-fi becomes real, essentially, sci-fi movies and TV shows become more normalized. And especially with all manner of sci-fi films having recently earned billions of dollars at the box office, it appears audiences, in general, are willing to give the genre more consideration, allowing sci-fi storytellers to take bigger risks in the process.

Ocarina Of Time Remake Confirms Once And For All That ReDead Are Corpses

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time remake has confirmed whether or not ReDead are actually corpses, after Super Smash Bros. turned it into a fierce debate.

As anyone who has played Majora’s Mask can attest to, The Legend of Zelda can have some pretty strong horror chops when it wants to. Ocarina of Time is a great example of this, as its chilling ReDead enemies are infamous for being far creepier than anything else Link runs into during his quest, and arguably more than in any other game.

There are plenty of things that people were excited to see in the Ocarina of Time remake, but the ReDead were easily near the top of that list since everyone wanted to know whether Nintendo would make them as faithfully terrifying as before. Now that the game is out, we know that’s definitely the case, and that a misconception started by Smash Bros. is incorrect.

Over on Twitter, maliceguardian started a since-deleted thread about what the ReDead actually are. While most would say they’re simply zombies or mummies, maliceguardian pointed to the remake’s design for them (particularly the mask) as proof that they’re “clay golems shaped like humans”. If you’re confused about how that theory came about, it’s actually the fault of Super Smash Bros. and its trophies.

We finally got a chance to play The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time remake and it's still one of the best games ever made.

In Melee, the ReDead trophy describes them as “just magic animated into hideous humanoid shapes”, while both the NTSC and Pal versions of Super Smash Bros. for Wii U and 3DS say they are “clay monsters fashioned in the shape of humans”. These trophies have caused many debates about whether ReDead are corpses, despite mainline Zelda games making it quite clear.

This clay monster description for the ReDead was adapted by Tri Force Heroes, which has them with a more plastic appearance and the ability to change shape.

After maliceguardian shared their thoughts on the matter, Twitter user VibeSharer put an end to the debate by pointing out that the Ocarina of Time remake’s Hall of Foes explicitly calls them “walking corpses with masks on their face”. As if that wasn’t concrete proof since it’s coming from the most recent Zelda game they appear in, AlTurkiei showed that you can even see their eyes behind the mask this time around.

I’m a little surprised that this was ever a debate, since Smash trophies aren’t exactly an encyclopedia of Nintendo canon and have been known to make mistakes in the past. At least we can finally put this long-standing misconception to rest and go back to being terrified of the ReDead instead of trying to explain them.
