Arkansas Bar Official Allegedly Transferred Points from Some Bar Examinees to Others

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Mostly law professors | Sometimes contrarian | Often libertarian | Always independent

From the Arkansas Supreme Court Thursday:

Evidence exists that Givens harvested points from a number of examinees' scores and then added them to other examinees' scores within the same test administration. Thus, she unilaterally elevated some examinees' scores to the minimum passing score of 270 or greater. Brech also identified at least one instance in which score alterations may have reduced an examinee's score-who otherwise would have passed-to a failing score. And in at least one instance, the individual who should have received the top score on the Arkansas Bar Examination had their score reduced thereby naming another examinee as the recipient of the highest score on the exam. If these allegations are true, these actions constitute an unprecedented breach of public trust.

An internal review by Robert Brech, Executive Director of the Supreme Court of Arkansas's Office of Professional Conduct and Programs, has uncovered extensive irregularities involving the Court's Office of Professional Programs and the administration of the Arkansas Bar Examination. The matter was turned over to state and federal authorities.

As of today, the Prosecuting Attorney's Office for the Sixth Judicial District filed formal criminal charges against Nancie Givens for 65 counts of tampering with a public record under Arkansas Code Annotated section 5-54-121 in connection with the alleged altering of numerous Arkansas Bar Examination scores and the deletion of files purportedly intended to conceal that misconduct. The charges followed a criminal investigation initiated in April 2026 by the Arkansas State Police and the Federal Bureau of Investigation. As always, all defendants are presumed innocent until proven guilty in a court of law.

Moving forward, we are committed to holding those involved accountable, taking continued corrective oversight of the Arkansas Bar Examination, and determining the appropriate course of action.

In February 2026, Givens, the former Executive Director of this Court's Office of Professional Programs, resigned. At that time, Robert Brech was serving as Executive Director of the Court's Office of Professional Conduct. Following Givens's resignation, the Court directed Brech to supervise the Office of Professional Programs, which oversees the administration of the Arkansas Bar Examination.

Upon his review of the Office of Professional Programs' records and administrative operations, Brech discovered that a large number of files and electronic communications were modified or deleted in early January 2026. Approximately 11,400 files were deleted or rendered unavailable. Most of those files included Arkansas Bar examinee applications, profiles, and examination score sheets that the Office of Professional Programs was required by this Court's rules to retain for twenty-five years.

Brech immediately notified a member of this Court, who directed him to determine the extent of the missing records and identify any other irregularities. On the basis of his review of the remaining files, Brech identified numerous discrepancies involving Arkansas Bar Examination scores across at least eight test administrations between July 2020 and July 2025. At that time, the matter was referred to the appropriate state and federal authorities.

Evidence exists that Givens harvested points from a number of examinees' scores and then added them to other examinees' scores within the same test administration. Thus, she unilaterally elevated some examinees' scores to the minimum passing score of 270 or greater. Brech also identified at least one instance in which score alterations may have reduced an examinee's score-who otherwise would have passed-to a failing score. And in at least one instance, the individual who should have received the top score on the Arkansas Bar Examination had their score reduced thereby naming another examinee as the recipient of the highest score on the exam. If these allegations are true, these actions constitute an unprecedented breach of public trust.

The vast majority of examinees who were previously informed that they had passed the Arkansas Bar Examination by the Office of Professional Programs did, in fact, pass the exam. Those examinees affected will be notified by the Office of Professional Conduct as soon as possible upon definitive confirmation of their status.

The Court is working with state and federal authorities to recover deleted and missing data, analyze all test administrations within that time frame, and determine the full scope of this grave and potentially criminal abuse of power. The people of Arkansas deserve, and the Court is committed to providing, total transparency in this matter. However, further disclosure will be forthcoming in a manner so as to not compromise the ongoing state and federal criminal investigations. Accordingly, additional disclosures will be limited as those efforts remain underway.

As an immediate step, independent of any state or federal criminal investigation or prosecution, Nancie Givens has been referred to the Office of Professional Conduct for investigation of extensive violations of the Arkansas Rules of Professional Conduct. See Ark. R. Prof. Conduct 8.1(a) & 8.4(a)-(e). Furthermore, new testing procedures and security measures were implemented with the February 2026 administration of the Arkansas Bar Examination to ensure systemic accountability and safeguard the integrity of the examination.

We recognize the profound consequences for affected examinees, the Bar, and the public at large. Our paramount concern is safeguarding litigants, the integrity of the legal profession, and public confidence in the administration of justice. While these matters remain under criminal and administrative investigation, we commit to pursue continued corrective measures in an effort to reestablish confidence in our institution.

The Buick Engine That Helped Launch The SR-71 Blackbird

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

The Lockheed SR-71 Blackbird spy plane is capable of flying 2,193.2 mph with a ceiling above 85,000 feet. To achieve those feats, it packed a pair of Pratt & Whitney J58 turbojets to enjoy a combined 50,000 pounds of thrust, and it even used a new kind of fuel that could withstand the friction from those blistering speeds. However, getting those mighty motors to turn over in the first place was no simple task, which is where the Buick Nailhead comes into play. It might not have directly powered the plane, but its earliest flights were only possible thanks to the engine.

A turbine engine requires a certain amount of airflow to ensure the fuel first ignites properly, and it has to reach a certain speed before the combustion process can sustain itself. As a result, the turbojet engines need to begin spinning even before they're operating on their own. But these are massive mills weighing in at 6,000 pounds each, so a simple electric starter like the one in a traditional car wouldn't have the strength to get the job done. A pair of Buick engines, on the other hand, would be enough to give its turbines the power they needed.

The mill used to get the Blackbird's own engines spinning was a 401-cubic-inch Nailhead, which can pound out 325 horses and 445 pound-feet of torque. The Air Force used a pair of those motors on the Blackbird's original start cart, yoking them together with a 12-inch-wide belt joining their transmissions. The combined output was then fed to the jets via mechanical linkage, spinning them up to their proper running speed. The pilot would then ignite the engines, allowing the Blackbird to run on its own from there.

We've previously explained how the Buick Nailhead V8 got its name; the tops of its vertically oriented valves looked like, well, nailheads. The first-gen motor from 1953 had a displacement of 322 cubic inches, and an example with 264 cubic inches came to be when the brand introduced the Buick Special the next year. That latter setup had a pretty short life, as it was out of production after 1955. The Nailheads then began their climb up the displacement ladder. From 1957 to 1961, Buick raised the bar to 364 cubic inches. There was also a 425-cubic-inch Nailhead V8 on the roster from 1963 to 1966, backed by 360 horsepower with its optional dual-quad carburetor setup.

The 401, though, was especially popular. Among the first cars with this engine under their hoods were the 1959 Buick Invicta and Electra. It's also worth noting that, in the Invicta, the motor gained the Wildcat 445 nickname for its impressive torque rating. The Invicta Wildcat that premiered in 1962 (and became the Buick Wildcat in 1963) relied on that mill to help boost the brand's performance credibility.

The striking first-generation Buick Riviera would boast the 401 Nailhead in 1963, as would the Skylark Gran Sport when it arrived in 1965. The Skylark GS represents the point where Buick went all-in on the 401's performance potential — at least from a marketing standpoint. Even though its engine's output was unchanged from previous Buicks, the Skylark GS is considered to be Buick's first muscle car. Production of the engine itself would meet its end by the next year, but its use in the Blackbird would continue for a much longer period of time.

The Buick Nailhead engines would go on to power plenty of dragsters, too, building a proven track record for delivering low-end torque. Remember, that Wildcat 445 exceeded a pound-foot of twist for every cubic inch of displacement. When you also consider the Nailhead's reputation for long-term reliability, it's no surprise that it worked so well on Blackbird duty.

Yet, after roughly a decade on the start cart, the Nailheads would be retired in the 1970s and replaced by two other power-packed GM V8 engines. These were 454-cubic-inch big-block LS7 V8s that had originally been engineered to bring 460 horses to the 1970 Chevrolet Corvette. A combination of circumstances kept that motor out of regular production entirely, but that didn't prevent the Air Force from snagging a pair. (As a side note, those LS7 big-blocks were unrelated to the more recent LS7 Corvette engine that defied small-block limits in the C6 Z06.)

Finally, in the 1980s, the Air Force would move to a technology that had been around since about three decades prior: the pneumatic starter. Rather than bringing the turbine up to operating speed with a direct mechanical connection, these work by forcing high-pressure air through the turbine blades — like blowing a stream of air across a pinwheel. The Blackbird relied on pneumatic starters from then on, until its retirement the 1990s.

Former ‘DWTS’ Contestant Danielle Fishel Says Judges Tougher On Female Contestants: “Men Get A Pass”

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Danielle Fishel, the former Dancing With The Stars contestant who got booted from the show last year, says female competitors generally have a tougher time winning over judges than male dancers.

On her podcast Ballroom Breakdown, Fishel, best known for her Boy Meets World role, was discussing DWTS‘ Horror Movie Night episode with her guest Kym Johnson. The two were responding to DWTS guest judge Shirley Ballas giving both Julia Stiles and Tatyana Ali scores of 4 when the judge had awarded Tyler Cameron a 5.

“I do believe that the judges, all of them, have a tendency to be harder on the women than they are on the male celebs,” Fishel said. “I could be wrong. But, in my opinion, I think sometimes the men get a pass that the women don’t get.” Related Stories News 'Dancing With The Stars' Season 35 Recap: Team Dances Shake Up The Competition On Disney Night News 'Dancing with the Stars' Pro Ezra Sosa Says "My Dream" Is For Same-Sex Dance Partner: "I'm Working My Magic!"

Watch the discussion below. Watch on Deadline

Johnson said she, too, was “surprised” that Ballas had preferred Cameron’s dance to the performances of Stiles and Ali, noting, “I thought the girls were better than that.”

Said Fishel, “I thought, ‘There’s no way that this dance was better than what we had seen. And so, if you were going to give the ladies 4s, I feel like [his] was worthy of a 4 as well.”

Fishel also said she thinks the pro-male bias can extend to audiences. “That same can be said for the audience at home, watching the judges,” she said. “The men get away with being able to give a constructive criticism here or there, whereas the women judges, Carrie Ann and the guest judges, they get booed.”

“So in general,” she concluded, “I think we see a little bit of unfairness with how women are treated in multiple different ways on the show.”

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Founder Summit begins Nov. 4, 9:00 a.m. ET. Get your pass savings & deals before it starts. REGISTER HERE.

Binance’s chief financial officer Kaiser Ng is leaving the cryptocurrency exchange.

According to Bloomberg, co-CEO Yi He said in a statement that Ng’s departure will allow him to “pursue a new venture after more than five years at Binance,” and that a transition plan has been “implemented over the last half year.”

The announcement comes barely more than a week after hackers stole $6.3 billion worth of cryptocurrency from Binance users. The company froze withdrawals for several days; after accounts were unfrozen, users withdrew a net $3.6 billion in the first few hours.

Binance and the FBI blamed a North Korean group called TraderTraitor for the hack. The company also said that it would cover all losses, promising, “Binance will bear the financial impact of the stolen funds. These losses will not be passed on to users.”

Ng will be replaced as CFO by Justin Yek.

Cold Seawater Is Supplying District Heat in Esbjerg, Denmark

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Esbjerg, Denmark, is using large electric heat pumps to extract heat from the North Sea and supply its district-heating network. During a two-hour winter test, seawater entered the system at 3.3°C and left at 1.2°C. The two heat pumps consumed 19.9 MW of electricity and supplied 55.2 MW of heat at a district-heating water temperature of 90°C. The difference, 35.3 MW, came from the seawater. The project team’s May 2025 operating report recorded a coefficient of performance, or COP, of 2.8. The figures describe that test, not annual average performance.

A heat pump transfers thermal energy from a colder source to a warmer destination. Its refrigerant absorbs heat at a temperature below that of the seawater, and an electrically driven compressor raises the temperature so the heat can enter the district network. Esbjerg uses carbon dioxide as its refrigerant in a high-pressure cycle. For each unit of electricity used by the compressors during the test, the plant delivered 2.8 units of heat. Resistance heating would have required 2.8 times as much electricity for the same heat output under those conditions.

The installation has a nominal heating capacity of 70 MW and began delivering heat in late 2024, as part of Esbjerg’s replacement of its retired coal-fired combined heat and power plant. The operating report describes controls for variable conditions and protection against freezing as seawater temperatures fall. The recorded COP of 2.8 was lower than the more favourable performance figures used in the original project expectations, and full guarantee testing had not been completed when the results were published. The city’s heating supply also includes a wood-chip boiler and waste-incineration heat. Emissions reductions reported for the wider replacement programme cannot be attributed in full to the seawater heat pumps.

Electricity price and heating-network temperature have a direct effect on the cost. At an illustrative electricity price of €100/MWh, compressor power at COP 2.8 costs about €36 per MWh of useful heat. That excludes seawater pumping, equipment maintenance, capital recovery, financing and heat distribution. A network able to operate at lower water temperatures generally asks the compressor to make a smaller temperature increase, which can improve COP. Older networks supplying 90°C heat face a harder operating requirement than newer, lower-temperature networks.

Esbjerg builds on decades of seawater heating experience. Monaco began using seawater heat pumps in 1963, and Stockholm has used large seawater-sourced district-heating equipment since the 1980s. Creative Energy’s Horseshoe Bay development in West Vancouver takes a different approach. Submerged heat exchangers transfer heat through a closed loop, with no seawater intake or discharge to the marina. Denmark’s Aalborg project is developing 177 MW of seawater heat-pump capacity. These installations serve different loads and use different marine arrangements, so their costs cannot be assumed to match Esbjerg’s.

Marine heat systems require more than suitable water temperatures. Engineers must address corrosion, biological fouling, intake and discharge effects, pumping requirements, maintenance access and freezing protection. The environmental assessment has to consider the local marine ecosystem and the discharge of colder water. The distribution network can be the larger financial obstacle in areas without existing pipes or concentrated heat demand. Wastewater, industrial waste heat, ground-source systems or individual air-source heat pumps may provide lower-cost heat at some sites.

Hot-water storage can add value by allowing a utility to make some heat when electricity is inexpensive and deliver it later. The available flexibility depends on storage capacity, network temperatures and heat demand. Esbjerg’s test provides direct evidence that cold seawater can support high-temperature district heating. Coastal utilities can use that evidence when comparing local heat sources, network investments and the costs of replacing combustion-based heating.

My TFIE Strategy Briefing assessment examines Esbjerg’s operating results, experience in Stockholm and Monaco, the Horseshoe Bay system near Vancouver, and the cost and environmental factors that determine where seawater heating is suitable.

Albert Camus on Stubbornness and Creativity

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Every month, I spend hundreds of hours and thousands of dollars keeping The Marginalian going. For two decades, it has remained free, ad-free, AI-free, fully human and alive thanks to patronage from readers. I have no staff, no interns, not even an assistant — a thoroughly one-woman labor of love that is also my life and my livelihood. If it makes your own life more livable in any way, please consider aiding its sustenance with a one-time or loyal donation. Your support makes all the difference.

Need to cancel a recurring donation? Go here.

For an act of resistance to the tyranny of algorithms, try the Marginalian newsletter—undistracted notes on the search for meaning, free, ad-free, AI-free, fully human since 2006. (Here is an example.)

Three years after he became the second-youngest laureate of the Nobel Prize, awarded him for literature that “with clear-sighted earnestness illuminates the problems of the human conscience,” Albert Camus (November 7, 1913–January 4, 1960) died in a car crash with an unused train ticket to the same destination in his pocket. The writings he left behind — about the key to strength of character, about creativity as resistance, about the antidotes to the absurdity of life, about happiness as our moral obligation — endure as a living testament to Mary Shelley’s conviction that “it is by words that the world’s great fight, now in these civilized times, is carried on.”

Camus addressed his views on writing most directly in a 1943 essay about the novel, included in his altogether indispensable Lyrical and Critical Essays (public library).

One must be two persons when one writes… The great problem is to translate what one feels into what one wants others to feel. We call a writer bad when he expresses himself in reference to an inner context the reader cannot know. The mediocre writer is thus led to say anything he pleases.

In a sentiment James Baldwin would echo in his advice on writing, insisting that “beyond talent lie all the usual words: discipline, love, luck, but most of all, endurance,” Camus observes that all creative endeavor demands of us “a certain constancy of soul, and a human and literary knowledge of sacrifice.” He writes:

To someone who asked Newton how he had managed to construct his theory, he could reply: “By thinking about it all the time.” There is no greatness without a little stubbornness.

Nearly a century after Tchaikovsky asserted that “a self-respecting artist must not fold his hands on the pretext that he is not in the mood,” Camus adds:

Great novels… prove the effectiveness of human creation. They convince one that the work of art is a human thing, never human enough, and that its creator can do without dictates from above. Works of art are not born in flashes of inspiration but in a daily fidelity.

Complement with more excellent advice on writing from Mary Oliver, Rachel Carson, Maya Angelou, George Saunders, John Steinbeck, and Ernest Hemingway, then revisit the beautiful letter of gratitude Camus sent to his childhood teacher shortly after receiving the Nobel Prize.

Charli XCX is ushering in the chilly season. On Thursday night, the Music, Fashion, Film artist was photographed outside of West Hollywood Italian restaurant Dan Tana’s, wearing an all-black look with a cozy pop of fur. She was at the Los Angeles hotspot for an event hosted by Khaite, which also saw new couple Dakota Johnson and Machine Gun Kelly, and selected a look full of the label’s fall staples.

The centerpiece of the monochrome fit was Khaite’s slouchy leather bomber jacket, crafted from super-soft lambskin leather with a sleek, shiny finish. The outerwear featured an oversize silhouette and a cropped hem, with ribbed trim that cinched the waist. It was stitched with a concealed zipper placket and adorned with a touch of brown shearling, which sat somewhere between an accent collar and a fur stole. XCX tucked her hair into the piece, and her hands into its angular welt pockets.

As for the rest of her look, the Grammy-winning singer went with a timeless pair of black straight-leg trousers, and slipped into Jimmy Choo’s Scarlett pumps: a pair of black leather pointed-toe shoes with a lace-up corset detail on the upper and a little drop heel. She added oversize shades from Khaite’s collaboration with eyewear brand Oliver Peoples, and finished things off with an envelope bag from her host.

The Khaite bag in question was defined by its lightweight feel and flat-body construction, which was designed to expand naturally when filled with a night’s worth of necessities.

Amazon’s new basic Kindle proves you don’t need all the bells and whistles

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now.

Founder Summit begins Nov. 4, 9:00 a.m. ET. Get your pass savings & deals before it starts. REGISTER HERE.

In October, Amazon announced its refreshed Kindle lineup, its first significant design overhaul in years. The highlights included a new edge-to-edge design that eliminated traditional raised bezels, thinner and lighter Paperwhite and Colorsoft models with better displays, and page-turning accessories like a Bluetooth remote and a magnetic cover with page-turn buttons.

However, given the impact of the global memory-chip shortage driven by the AI boom, the new Kindles also debuted with pricing that had increased by $40 to $50 across the lineup. That makes it more difficult for the average consumer to consider buying one of the more premium devices.

For that reason, the Kindle I most wanted to get my hands on was the 6-inch entry-level device, which starts at $169.99 for the ad-free version. With the price hikes, this is the Kindle that will remain the most affordable to new e-reader buyers.

The question I weighed was whether or not the basic Kindle is even worth buying at this price when there are arguably better Kindles on the market. The answer, after a couple of weeks of testing, is yes — if you can live without the bells and whistles and prefer a smaller, lighter device.

On the 6-inch Kindle, there’s no warm light to reduce eye strain, no color display, no auto-adjusting brightness, no option to use the page-turn cover, and it’s not waterproof. Obviously, because of its size, the screen is smaller, as is the battery. Battery life is 6 weeks compared with the Paperwhite’s 12 weeks or Colorsoft’s 8 weeks.

Still, even this basic Kindle has seen improvements of its own, mostly from a physical design perspective.

The plastic 2026 Kindle is 11% lighter and 14% thinner than the 2024 model, weighing 140g versus 158g and measuring 6.85mm thick versus 8mm. While this seems like a small change, the 18-gram difference is noticeable and particularly welcome for a device you’re likely to carry in your purse or bag. Plus, it makes it easier to hold for hours on end when you’re really binging your way through a book.

(The aluminum version, meanwhile, is slightly heavier at 162g, or 4 grams more than the 2024 Kindle, though it still benefits from the slimmer design.)

And despite being the “starter” Kindle, the 6-inch basic device still received the new flush-front screen update. This makes it more comfortable to hold in your hand, especially for those long reading sessions. Amazon explained that you don’t have to worry about no longer having a bezel to rest your thumb upon, either, as the new Kindles have been updated to detect a dead zone in the capacitive touch that will readjust to account for the fact that your finger is there.

In practice, that doesn’t mean you can just press your thumb down anywhere on the Kindle’s screen as you hold it — doing so would register this as an attempt to highlight or look up a word. Instead, it means you can rest your thumb or fingers on the outer edge of the Kindle’s screen (the colored border) without triggering an unintentional tap, even though some of your finger will be touching part of the Kindle’s capacitive screen.

In terms of the software, the new basic Kindle is a bit snappier than its 2024 predecessor. This is most noticeable when opening books, which Amazon claims to be 30% faster.

If you’re coming from the 2019 Kindle Basic, the 2026 version is an even bigger upgrade, as you gain USB-C, more storage (from 4GB to 8GB to now 16GB or 32GB), the new flush screen, and the screen resolution jumps from 167 ppi to 300 ppi. It’s faster, thinner, and lighter, too, and the battery lasts weeks longer.

Another perk about the basic Kindle beyond its small size (this thing fits in your jeans’ back pocket!), is that you can now pay a little more for an upgraded version with an aluminum backing instead of plastic and more storage. The new $189.99 aluminum option comes in Seagrass and Alpine Sky hues, and ships with 32GB of storage. Meanwhile, the $169.99 plastic model offers 16GB of storage.

However, even if you go for the less expensive option, you can at least now pick a fun color other than black (aka Graphite), as Amazon has introduced a cheery purple option called Ube. (I almost wish I’d gone for this one, just for the whimsy of it, even though I prefer the feel of the aluminum.)

I should point out that you can use the Kindle Click, the new Bluetooth remote for page turning, with the basic Kindle, but I’m not sure why you’d need to, if not for some accessibility-related reason. The 6-inch Kindle was already the smallest, and now it’s lighter and easier to hold, too. (But perhaps I just don’t get the appeal of the remote? I’ve barely used the remote with my Kobo either!)

The big thing to consider here is whether you want to spend this much on a Kindle that lacks a warm light. While this is something of a personal preference, for some readers, no warm light is a dealbreaker. The warm light shifts the color tone of the Kindle’s display from a cooler bluish-white to a softer amber glow, which is meant to help reduce eye strain and improve reading conditions in low light or at night.

Whether you need this feature depends on where you like to read and how long your sessions tend to be. If you read nightly in bed with little light, you might prefer paying extra for a $199.99 16GB Kindle Paperwhite. (That version only comes in Graphite. The 32GB Paperwhite can come in Seaglass, too, but is $249.99).

If you often read by the pool or on vacation, you also might want to upgrade to a waterproof Kindle.

But if you can live without the extra features and want the best purse- or pocket-sized device, the 6-inch Kindle is still a viable option.

Costco Customers Order The Chocolate Cake With This One Adjustment For A Massive Flavor Boost

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

"Like a gigantic, delicious Hostess Suzy Q," one commenter on a Reddit thread devoted to this swap said (we can almost hear their mouth watering through the internet). Another Costco customer wrote that the employee at their bakery suggested they do this for their daughter's birthday cake, and the results were so, so good. "Rave reviews from the party guests," they declared.

If you can find a Costco bakery to pull off this feat of cake deliciousness (more on that later), it stands to reason you could also request the inverse. If you're not a huge fan of chocolate cake (or you're serving those that aren't), but you do enjoy chocolate mousse filling, you have options!

It seems that you used to be able to order this opposite-flavor combination cake on the actual order form at the Costco bakery, but that is no longer the case. In fact, you might not be able to get a chocolate cake with vanilla cheesecake mousse filling (or vice versa) at all if your bakery doesn't allow for this customization, which some very likely might not. So how can you be sure? While there are no direct phone lines to the individual bakeries, you could try calling the store and requesting that someone ask the bakery on your behalf.

If you have received a response in the affirmative (or perhaps, you know others who have already obtained cakes like these from your location), it's also in your best interest to place the order at least a week ahead of when you'll need it, to give the bakery plenty of time to make the necessary adjustments. If you're making the cake arrangements the old-fashioned, and frankly outdated, way in-store on the form, where it reads, "Filled with 2 lbs. X mousse," you can just cross out the flavor of mousse you don't want, and write in the flavor you do want.

If you're taking advantage of Costco's new cake ordering upgrade on its app, however, you might instead include a message about the desired swap in the Notes section at the end of the form. It's just important to remember that whichever way you choose, it's of tantamount importance that you include your phone number, so the bakery can reach out to confirm details if needed.

Arsenal 1-0 Hull City: Martin Ødegaard rescues Gunners with late winner at the Emirates

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Lucie Turner writes about Arsenal for Read Arsenal and Newcastle United for Read Newcastle. A long-time Arsenal supporter, she also follows rugby, snooker, darts and Formula 1. Her work covers match previews and reports, breaking news, player profiles and opinion pieces.

Arsenal needed a moment of quality from their captain to overcome Hull City and claim a narrow 1-0 Premier League victory at the Emirates Stadium.

Martin Ødegaard scored in the 72nd minute as the hosts struggled to break down a resolute Hull side. It was much like I expected in my match preview, as Arsenal had to work hard to win this game.

The result gives them an extended lead at the top of the table. Ahead of Manchester City’s clash with Nottingham Forest. However, Mikel Arteta will be aware that his team did not play well in this game. It was far from a walk in the park.

Hull arrived at London looking to play an attacking game. Jakirovic’s side were well organised and looked to launch quick counter attacks.

They did have some positive moments in the first half, but Arsenal struggled to get into their attacking rhythm. Kai Havertz couldn’t capitalise on one good chance created by Ødegaard.

The hosts were hopeful of taking the lead after Havertz fell to the ground in the box after being pulled back by Nobel Mendy.

However, referee Farai Hallam’s initial decision was overturned after a VAR review showed Havertz was offside earlier in the attack, so Arsenal’s penalty was cancelled.

Another frustrating moment for the home side, who failed to score during the first half, despite dominating possession.

Arsenal came out strong after the break. However, Hull were still causing problems. Lewie Coyle was also walking a fine line after a yellow card in the first half and a late challenge on Christos Tzolis.

Arteta made a three-man change on the hour mark, bringing on Viktor Gyökeres and Mikel Merino for Havertz and Declan Rice. Six minutes later he brought on Eberechi Eze and Piero Hincapié.

The breakthrough came from Madueke, forcing Konstantinos Tzolakis into action. The Hull keeper couldn’t hold his shot, and Ødegaard was quickest to react, scoring from the rebound.

The captain’s strike took the frustration out of Arsenal’s efforts and was a reward for their perseverance. It also provided another vital moment for the Norwegian. He’s had a tough campaign so far due to injuries.

Despite going behind, Hull never gave up in their search for an equaliser. However, Arsenal held on to their lead.

Arsenal dominated the game with 67% of possession and 21 shots, compared to just seven for Hull. Tzolakis put in a number of key interventions to keep the scoreline respectable.

Although they weren’t at their best, Arsenal were able to perform in the key moments of the game. Ødegaard’s goal meant that the Gunners earned three points and are sitting top of the Premier League heading into the international break.

ATOM is holding near the lower boundary of a long-term descending channel, with chart support around $1.23–$1.385.

Paxos has listed ATOM, allowing fintechs and brokerages on its platform to offer the token to customers.

Cosmos Hub approved the arrangement through Proposal 1054, with costs covered by the community pool and no monthly fees.

Cosmos has spent years grinding lower, but the weekly chart is showing a potentially important test. The ATOM price is holding near the lower boundary of a long-term descending channel, with support around $1.23 to $1.385. Now, a Paxos listing gives the token a fresh fundamental development to work with.

The chart shows ATOM trading near the channel’s lower boundary after an extended decline. The marked support area sits around $1.23 to $1.385, making this zone important for the current setup.

Still, holding support isn’t the same as confirming a reversal. The descending channel remains in place, and buyers need to demonstrate that the latest bounce has staying power.

The bigger development comes from Paxos. Its listing of ATOM enables fintech companies and brokerages on its platform to offer the token to their customers through existing infrastructure.

That’s useful distribution. It gives participating businesses a route to provide ATOM access without building an entirely separate system themselves. Whether that translates into meaningful demand remains to be seen.

Cosmos Hub governance approved the arrangement through Proposal 1054. The proposal covers the full cost from the community pool, with no monthly fees.

That removes a recurring payment obligation under the approved arrangement. But the listing itself doesn’t guarantee increased buying or a sustained price recovery.

The chart marks potential upside levels at $2.10, $3.60 and $5.50, followed by $10 and $15.50. These are conditional targets, not confirmed destinations.

For now, the ATOM price needs to defend its lower-channel support and establish a convincing bounce. Paxos adds a new access route, but the chart still has to do the talking.

Bitcoin traders could mistake this Binance data change for a rush to sell

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Coin Metrics’ November 4 notice complicates Binance balance signals as traders look for evidence of demand before CPI.

Bitcoin is trading near $76,000 ahead of Tuesday's US inflation report, while reported spot selling puts the strength of demand in focus. A warning from data provider Coin Metrics complicates one apparent signal: a larger reported Binance wallet balance can reflect expanded address coverage without fresh coins arriving.

Bitcoin was quoted at $75,871 on CryptoSlate's price page when captured at 10:42 UTC on Nov. 7. That puts $76,000 in view as a rounded market price, rather than an established technical support level.

CryptoSlate's Nov. 7 report cited CryptoQuant analyst Darkfost describing more aggressive spot selling than buying on Binance and Coinbase. Coin Metrics' coverage notice concerns a different measurement and does not invalidate that trading signal.

The upcoming inflation release gives traders a defined window to compare potential selling with actual demand. A balance chart alone cannot show whether holders have just moved coins onto an exchange, or sold them.

In its Nov. 4 notice, Coin Metrics said it planned to update exchange-address data after additional addresses were disclosed. It anticipated significant increases in some hourly supply metrics that day and daily metrics on Nov. 5, particularly for Binance BTC measures in native units and US dollars.

The provider said the planned changes would reflect broader address coverage without a corresponding change in actual exchange holdings. The notice supplies a reason to question an apparent jump, but it does not quantify one.

Coin Metrics' exchange-supply methodology counts balances in identified hot and cold wallets at the end of an interval. It also warns that undiscovered addresses can leave the measure below actual holdings. Identifying an additional wallet therefore changes what the dataset sees, without establishing that a customer has just deposited more Bitcoin.

Dollar-denominated supply adds another moving part: it is calculated from the coin balance and the USD price. A rise in that dollar reading alone cannot isolate an increase in the number of coins.

The next step is to examine deposits, which Coin Metrics defines as assets sent to an exchange during an interval, using addresses it identifies as exchange-owned. Those observations measure transfers. They do not establish that the transferred coins were sold.

Comparability matters here too. Coin Metrics' point-in-time documentation says standard exchange histories can be revised when new addresses are discovered. Point-in-time histories keep past values fixed, but newly discovered addresses still enter coverage from their discovery date onward. Choosing that series does not automatically make every date comparable.

Evidence of execution comes from a different dataset. Coin Metrics' trade records include time, price and amount, plus the taker's side where known. A sell taker is an incoming order that removes a resting bid. That identifies aggressive selling, rather than merely coins held at an exchange.

Inflation news could change expectations for monetary policy, with Treasury yields and appetite for risk among the possible transmission channels. The Federal Reserve's explanation describes how expected policy changes can affect longer-term rates and asset prices. These are possible channels for the coming release, rather than a prediction of Bitcoin's direction.

For this demand test, comparable flows would show whether coins are moving onto exchanges, while executed spot trades would show whether sellers are executing against standing buy orders. The price response would then help assess whether buyers are absorbing that pressure: aggressive selling alongside a resilient price would be consistent with absorption; sustained price weakness would make that reading harder to defend.

These observations need to cover comparable periods and measurements. They would not, by themselves, connect a particular depositor with a particular sale. For holders watching the inflation release, the useful signal is how actual selling meets demand, with Binance's changing wallet coverage accounted for first.

Where children live may influence how well they focus

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Children in greener neighborhoods scored slightly higher on attention tests, suggesting nearby trees and parks may play a role in focus.

Children often do better on attention tasks after a walk in the park or time in a school garden.

But researchers have had mixed answers about whether living near trees and parks is also linked to better focus.

A large study of American children now finds a small connection between neighborhood greenery and attention.

Links with reading and vocabulary were less clear after researchers accounted for family income.

Carina Fowler led the study at Washington University in St. Louis. She is a postdoctoral researcher in the lab of Deanna Barch, a professor in the Department of Psychological and Brain Sciences.

“If a child lives closer to green space, and has more access to green space, does this help them pay attention on demand when it’s required, and do they do better at school on achievement tests?” Fowler said.

The researchers explored an idea called attention restoration theory. It suggests that nature gives the mind a break from the effort of staying focused.

The theory describes two kinds of attention: one we use to concentrate on a task, and another that responds automatically to things around us.

Phones, TVs and traffic can grab that automatic attention completely, while trees and parks may hold it gently enough to let the mind wander.

This may give our ability to concentrate a chance to recover.

Fowler said earlier research suggests people dislike having nothing to see or hear, or sitting alone with their thoughts in a quiet room.

“It seems people need something to be occupying them a little bit – but not too much – and that nature can provide that really well,” Fowler said.

The team used data from the Adolescent Brain Cognitive Development Study, which recruited children near 21 sites across the continental United States.

The analysis included 11,868 children who joined between 2016 and 2018, aged nine or 10.

For each child’s home, the team studied the surrounding census tract, an area used in the national census that is roughly the size of a neighborhood.

Satellite images showed how much land trees covered.

Records checked by the Trust for Public Land showed how much land was parkland. The researchers averaged the tree and park percentages to give each child a green space score.

Across the group, the combined score averaged about 11%.

The children took five short tests that involved remembering things, switching between rules, ignoring distractions and processing information quickly.

The team combined the results into an attention score.

The score used a scale similar to an IQ test, with 100 in the middle and a typical spread of about 15 points.

Reading and vocabulary tests produced a second score that gave a rough picture of school skills.

The team looked at each child at one point in time. That means the results can show a connection, but cannot prove that greenery caused higher scores.

Children’s focus depends on many things. The researchers considered sex and how well off each neighborhood was.

They also included ADHD, stimulant medicines, sleep problems, weekly screen time and tobacco exposure before birth.

Other checks covered air pollution, physical activity and whether children felt safe from crime in their neighborhood.

According to the authors, no earlier study of green space and attention had included children’s own sense of safety.

“You have to feel safe in the green space to have an effect,” Fowler said.

After those checks, a 10-percentage-point increase in green space was linked to an attention score 0.76 points higher. Reading and vocabulary scores were 0.83 points higher.

A 10-percentage-point rise would mean going from 10% to 20%, for example.

The score differences were small: less than one point on a scale with a typical spread of 15 points.

Many children had little greenery nearby. About half lived in areas with a green space score below 10%, and about one in 10 had a score below 1%.

The authors suggested that even a small increase in greenery might matter in those places. Attention and school skills at this age can help predict later health, earnings and education.

Greener neighborhoods tend to have cleaner air, and studies have linked air pollution to attention problems. The team checked whether tiny pollution particles could explain some of the results.

These particles measure no more than about one ten-thousandth of an inch across, or 2.5 micrometers.

Lower particle pollution explained a small part of the link with both scores, which surprised the authors because they had expected it to explain none.

“There is something to the idea that air quality is partially explaining why green space is helping our attention,” Fowler said.

Exercise did not explain the link. Children in greener areas did not report more days with at least an hour of activity, although more active children scored higher on both measures.

The team then added family income to the analysis, which already included how well off the neighborhood was.

The link between greenery and attention became about half as large, but it remained.

The link with reading and vocabulary was no longer statistically significant. This makes it harder to tell how much greenery itself might matter for those skills.

The authors offered two possible explanations. Green space may have a weaker link with school skills, or it may help focus sooner while any effect on learning takes years to build.

The attention score combined several skills, so it was not a test of attention alone.

The school skills score covered reading and vocabulary, leaving out math, science and writing.

The study also included relatively few rural children because most of its brain-imaging centers are in cities.

And the team measured greenery at only one point in each child’s life, so it could not show what happens after a move to a greener area.

The authors suggested testing whether greener school grounds help children learn.

They also raised the possibility that keeping children inside during recess as punishment for poor focus or grades could make those problems worse.

Both ideas need testing, as does whether planting trees near homes or schools improves attention.

Experiments and studies that follow children over many years could help answer those questions.

The study is published in the journal Environment International.

Roma meet with Alejandro Balde’s agent for January

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Roma are eyeing Barcelona for the January transfer window. And Tony D’Amico has Alejandro Balde in his sights.

The Spanish left-back could leave the Blaugrana as early as the next transfer window, and the Giallorossi want to capitalize on this.

According to Spanish newspaper SPORT, the sporting director has already met with Jorge Mendes, the player’s agent, to determine whether there are opportunities to bring him to the capital.

Meanwhile, things are moving at Barcelona. Flick would like to strengthen the defense with a new center-back, but the club’s financial difficulties would necessitate at least one departure.

And Balde, who has played just 124 minutes this season, is reportedly among the leading candidates to leave.

The Spanish club’s asking price is around €40 million, although the possibility of a loan with an obligation to buy, subject to certain conditions, is gaining traction.

The road, however, is far from clear. In the Premier League, Liverpool, Aston Villa, and Manchester United are monitoring the situation, without having yet submitted official offers. Juventus has also set its sights on the full-back, but in Italy, Roma are reportedly keen to make a move.

Gasperini particularly appreciates the Spaniard’s qualities, and D’Amico has reportedly already begun exploring the possibility of an agreement. The main issue, even more than the transfer fee, remains Balde’s salary. Mendes is reportedly evaluating various options for his client, with a final decision expected in December. Roma, meanwhile, is waiting, ready to play its cards if the financial conditions for the deal to close are met.

Martin Ødegaard extends Arsenal lead at top with winner to break Hull resistance

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

As most Arsenal supporters would testify, patience is a virtue. They needed plenty during the 22-year wait to be crowned Premier League champions and Mikel Arteta’s side certainly had to retain their composure as they tried to break down a dogged Hull side. They had not beaten Arsenal in 14 attempts since winning on their first visit to this ground in 2008. But with 20 minutes to play, there was still every chance that Sergej Jakirovic’s team could have pulled off one of the shocks of the season.

Arsenal had already been denied a penalty just before half-time for a shirt pull on Kai Havertz in the box a week after escaping the same crime against Liverpool at Anfield, in a moment of controversy that could have been the major talking point from this match. Martin Ødegaard had other ideas and the Arsenal captain’s fifth goal of the season was enough to ensure they will go into the international break with a healthy lead over Manchester City. Enzo Maresca’s side are up next here in the league after the international break, with Arsenal having matched their record tally of 27 points from their opening 10 games. The signs look ominous for the rest, even if they were made to work for the points throughout by their opponents.

“To the international break after winning is one of the best things for a coach,” said Arteta. “I need to praise the players and all the staff for the work that they are doing because it’s been very, very demanding. We have a very short time to recover from games but despite that the mentality and the quality of the team has shown: it’s incredible.”

Jakirovic had promised beforehand that his side would go on the front foot. “If you are passive, they will destroy you,” said the Bosnian. But while his bold 5-3-2 formation caused Arsenal’s much-vaunted defence some issues, Hull remain without a league victory since the end of August. “It’s a big experience for us,” was Jakirovic’s post-match assessment. “We can take some big lessons from this but we have shown we can compete at this level.”

Hull made the brighter start as John Egan headed wide from a corner. After losing to Brighton before the last international break, Arteta was taking no chances and reverted to the side that won at Liverpool last weekend. There was no William Saliba on the bench, with the France defender set to play for the under-21s on Sunday instead as he continues his comeback from a back injury.

In the Guardian app, tap the Profile settings button at the top right, then select Notifications. Turn on sport notifications.

If you already have the Guardian app, make sure you’re on the most recent version.

If you don't have the Guardian app, download it from the iOS App Store on iPhone or the Google Play store on Android by searching for 'The Guardian'.

Yet aside from a set piece that resulted in Gabriel Magalhães just failing to connect with Ezri Konsa’s flick-on, the visitors’ low block frustrated Arsenal and they continued to cause problems on the break. Elliot Stroud forced David Raya into a save after Riccardo Calafiori surrendered possession cheaply.

Havertz’s failure to take a shot after being played in by a slide-rule pass from Ødegaard drew groans of frustration. A toiling Arsenal thought all their prayers had been answered just before half-time when the referee, Farai Hallam, was sent to the big screen by the VAR. Nobel Mendy was clearly pulling Havertz’s shirt inside the area yet the German was offside so the penalty was not awarded, even though it probably should have been according to the rules. They state that Havertz must be “attempting to play the ball, or challenging an opponent for the ball” to be penalised and because Mendy’s offence came first, that should have taken precedence, as it did when Newcastle’s Fabian Schär was awarded a penalty against Bournemouth in February 2024.

Pro Ref has yet to make a statement on the incident and Arteta admitted he had been left confused by the whole episode. “I don’t know what the interpretation is, I’m sure they will make that clear to understand,” he said.

The Arsenal manager’s annoyance levels continued to rise when Havertz could only head straight at Konstantinos Tzolakis on the stroke of half-time. Hull came out for the second half with a similar attitude and Jakirovic picked up a yellow card for protesting against another decision that went against his team. Declan Rice was withdrawn early, although Arteta insisted he was fine afterwards. Noni Madueke found space after a flowing Arsenal move but again his effort was straight down the throat of Tzolakis.

The feeling of desperation was growing. But Ødegaard has made a habit of coming to Arsenal’s rescue this season and the captain was quickest to react when Tzolakis spilled Madueke’s shot to finally break Hull’s brave resistance.

Player Ratings: Blackburn 2-1 West Ham as Hermansen Has a Nightmare

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

West Ham’s promising start at Ewood Park ultimately counted for very little as Scott Parker’s side slipped to a 2-1 defeat against Blackburn Rovers. Despite enjoying plenty of possession and creating opportunities, the Hammers were undone by poor defending and some particularly questionable goalkeeping.

Joël Piroe’s late goal gave West Ham a chance of rescuing something, but the longer the game went on, the worse we seemed to play. There were some decent individual performances, particularly early on, but others will want to forget this afternoon in a hurry.

A really poor afternoon for the goalkeeper. I thought he could have been far more commanding for Blackburn’s opening goal, whether that meant catching the cross, punching it or simply getting something on the ball. He did make a straightforward late save from Todd Cantwell, but that doesn’t change my overall assessment.

One of West Ham’s more reliable performers before being substituted. Made some useful defensive interceptions and regularly pushed forward to support Bowen down the right. Tried a couple of long throws into the penalty area, although they didn’t really cause Blackburn much trouble.

Had some positive moments going forward, including a driving run from defence and a header that went narrowly wide early in the second half. However, I thought he could have done better for Blackburn’s first goal, and his performance deteriorated badly towards the end. Was fortunate not to concede a penalty before slipping twice during a dreadful passage of play that handed Blackburn a dangerous counter-attack.

Probably West Ham’s most dependable defender on the day. Made several important interceptions, particularly down the left where Blackburn repeatedly threatened, and was willing to carry the ball forward to help build attacks. Also produced a decent cross for Castellanos, although the resulting header went wide.

A mixed afternoon for the youngster. I was impressed with his willingness to get forward, his passing combinations with Austbø and some of the crosses he delivered. However, Blackburn repeatedly targeted his side, often leaving him facing two opponents, and he was beaten too easily in the build-up to the opening goal. Still very much learning the position.

Didn’t think he had a particularly good game. Had plenty of involvement, took the corners and got himself into shooting positions, but too often the final product was disappointing. Lost possession unnecessarily on occasions, failed to close down Blackburn’s second goalscorer quickly enough and wasted a promising late free kick with poor delivery.

You certainly couldn’t fault his work rate. At times he seemed to be everywhere, covering at left-back, appearing on the right and getting forward to support the attack. However, he wasn’t nearly as influential on the ball as he had been during the second half against Cardiff, and his shooting was disappointing. Replaced by Castellanos after 64 minutes.

One of West Ham’s more inventive players, particularly during the first half. Looked to move the ball quickly, attempted some clever through passes and wasn’t afraid to shoot, forcing one save and seeing another effort deflected behind. Not everything worked, but at least he was trying to make things happen.

I was genuinely surprised when he was substituted because he’d been one of West Ham’s livelier attacking players. Produced a lovely dribble past two defenders in the first half, linked up well with Mayers and delivered an excellent cross that Bowen headed against the post. Could perhaps have offered Mayers more defensive protection before Blackburn’s opener, but I thought he was contributing plenty going forward.

Blackburn 2-1 West Ham: Austbo was one of the Hammers better players

Worked hard to make things happen and was heavily involved in West Ham’s attacking play, particularly from the right. Unlucky to see his header strike the post following Austbø’s cross, while a difficult chance from close range went wide as he was falling and sliding. Also produced a lovely chipped delivery for Souček’s late header, but couldn’t quite provide the decisive contribution West Ham needed.

A frustrating performance rescued to some extent by an excellent goal. Too often he seemed hesitant in promising positions, allowing defenders to recover, while one particularly poor pass in the second half wasted a good attacking opportunity. However, he reacted brilliantly to Castellanos’ cross in the 82nd minute, firing high into the roof of the net. Whatever else you say about his performance, he took that chance extremely well.

Replaced Austbø after 58 minutes and was practically anonymous. Considering how involved Austbø had been, the substitution certainly didn’t improve West Ham’s attacking threat down the left.

Introduced for Kanté after 64 minutes and provided the cross that led to Piroe’s goal. Also had a header go wide from a Kilman delivery and was involved in the scramble that presented Bowen with a difficult chance. At least contributed something tangible to the attack.

Didn’t have a great deal of time to influence proceedings, particularly with West Ham having already lost their shape. Played one very silly ball into Orford inside our own penalty area, creating unnecessary danger. However, he also got into one of those familiar goalscoring positions in stoppage time, only to direct his header straight at the goalkeeper from Bowen’s excellent delivery.

Another substitute who came into a team that had lost its shape and was struggling for confidence. Was put in a difficult position by Souček’s poor pass inside the penalty area and was fortunate not to concede a penalty. Difficult to judge him too harshly given the limited time he had on the pitch and the state of the game when he entered.

Apple Completes the Set: Every Magnificent Seven Stock Opts Out of OKX-ICE Venue

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Apple Completes the Set: Every Magnificent Seven Stock Opts Out of OKX-ICE Venue

Every member of Wall Street’s “Magnificent Seven” now wants its stock kept off OKX and ICE’s planned tokenized stock venue.

Apple and Meta Platforms are among 11 companies added to the list of objecting issuers in an updated notice from OKXICE TSV dated Thursday.

They join Microsoft, Alphabet, Amazon, Nvidia and Tesla, completing a sweep by the group of tech giants that makes up a large share of the S&P 500’s value. The other new objectors are Home Depot, Boeing, GE Vernova, Palantir, ServiceNow, S&P Global, KLA, Super Micro Computer and Nebius Group, bringing the total number of companies saying “no” to the OKX and ICE platform to 67.

Meta’s objection carries some irony. The Facebook parent once tried to launch its own stablecoin, Libra, later renamed Diem, before abandoning the project in 2022 amid regulatory pushback.

Apple had been one of the last megacaps missing from the list after Amazon, Tesla, and SpaceX objected in the venue’s Nov. 2 notice. The tally stood at 56 then, and at nine on Oct. 15, when the first objectors appeared.

Just as notable is who hasn’t objected. Crypto-friendly public companies such as Coinbase, Robinhood and Strategy don’t appear on the list. Coinbase launched tokenized stocks for non-U.S. users on its Base network in August. Berkshire Hathaway, Visa, and Exxon Mobil are also absent. It’s unclear whether OKXICE notified those companies.

Under the SEC’s innovation exemption, which the agency unveiled in September after the Clarity Act failed to advance in the Senate, a venue must notify a company before offering a version of its shares tokenized by an unaffiliated third party. If the company objects in writing within 30 days, the venue can’t make that stock available for trading, according to the SEC’s order. It’s worth noting, though, that the objection applies only to that venue.

OKXICE TSV, a 50-50 joint venture between NYSE owner Intercontinental Exchange and OKX’s U.S. holding company, plans to offer tokenized U.S. stocks 24/7 through permissioned pools on OKX’s XLayer blockchain. An outside “tokenizer” would hold real shares one-for-one through a registered broker-dealer.

With the market’s biggest names opted out, it’s unclear which major stocks the venue will be able to offer at launch, and its notices don’t list the tokens it plans to make available.

Art, philosophy, and science blend in beautiful wildlife photography

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Published Nov 7, 2026 5:01 PM EST

Excerpted from UNITED NATURE by Andrew Zuckerman. Copyright © 2026 by Andrew Zuckerman. Published in the United States by Crown, an imprint of the Crown Publishing Group, a division of Penguin Random House LLC.

For as long as I can remember, I have made images. Much of my childhood was spent in the darkroom in my parents’ basement, surrounded by the smell of chemicals and the magic of forms slowly appearing on paper. Of all the things I’ve pursued in my life, the camera has been the one constant. It has shaped how I see: a device that slows me down, compels me to look more deeply and to understand the subjects on an essential level. And for me, the subject has always been other forms of life.

In that sense, I am part of a long tradition. The very first images humans made were not of themselves, but of other beings. Almost forty thousand years ago, in the caves of Chauvet in France, someone painted lions, rhinoceroses, mammoths, and bears with striking naturalism, allowing the animals to emerge from the contours and relief of the stone walls. Nearly twenty thousand years later, in the caves of Lascaux, we find more painted walls: herds of horses in motion and bison colliding, painted with sophisticated perspective and composition. Humans have never stopped depicting other beings. In Egypt, cats and jackals accompanied the dead into eternity. Dolphins appear again and again on Greek pottery. In medieval manuscripts, we find lions and owls used to represent human virtues and vices. Enlightenment engravers focused on the natural world, seeking order through detail. A generation later, John James Audubon’s The Birds of America presented perhaps the most realistic natural history illustration: life-size depictions of herons, warblers, and owls, rendered with exquisite precision.

With the arrival of photography two centuries ago, the human act of depicting the natural world shifted profoundly. This was not just a new tool, it was a rupture: the first time nature could enact itself with a fidelity and presence previously unseen. The world’s first published book of photographs arrived in 1843: Photographs of British Algae: Cyanotype Impressions by Anna Atkins. Atkins treated paper with a chemical solution, added specimens onto the treated paper, and left them under sunlight. This produced a white silhouette against a blue background. Atkins created these pictures of algae at the height of botanical illustration. While her peers were exhibiting artistic mastery over nature, she was removing herself. Here, the algae left its own impression directly on the paper. The plant depicted itself. The plant had agency.

One year after Atkins’s algae cyanotypes, William Henry Fox Talbot published The Pencil of Nature, a book containing twenty-four calotypes, the first paper negative, which allowed multiple positive prints to be made from a single negative. Photography was so new that Talbot felt required to include a notice: “The plates of the present work are impressed by the agency of Light alone, without any aid whatsoever from the artist’s pencil. They are the sun-pictures themselves, and not, as some persons have imagined, engravings in imitation.” Like Atkins, Talbot emphasized that the image was not mediated by the hand of the artist but drawn directly by nature itself—“sun-pictures” made by light. These early inventors of photography were introducing a new way of observing and recording the living world—one in which nature was no longer simply depicted, but allowed to speak for itself.

Only a few years later, in 1859, Charles Darwin published On the Origin of Species, advancing the radical idea that all life was in flux and that variation, adaptation, and transformation were the fundamental conditions of existence. In this light, Atkins’s cyanotypes seem almost prophetic: life depicted not as a fixed emblem to be mastered, but as a living process.

Skipping forward to the late nineteenth century, Cherry Kearton was pioneering what is now considered wildlife photography by hiding in hollowed-out ox carcasses or building bird-shaped blinds to get close to subjects. In the early twentieth century, photography extended into motion. Eadweard Muybridge’s studies of horses and birds revealed the invisible mechanics of gait and flight, moments too fast for the human eye to clock. Jacques Cousteau created ocean documentaries that gave the general population access to and intimacy with distant species.

By the late twentieth century, nature photography and film had become not only a way of seeing, but also a way of caring—shaping public consciousness about endangered species, vanishing habitats, and the fragility of ecosystems.

This notion of the image as a tool to influence our thinking found perhaps its most profound expression in the late 1960s, when Stewart Brand lobbied NASA to release the first photographs of the whole Earth seen from space. When those images finally appeared on the cover of his Whole Earth Catalog, they offered a new planetary perspective: not a map or an illustration, but the living sphere itself. For the first time, humanity could see Earth as a singular organism.

That one image—blue oceans swirling with white clouds, landmasses without borders—reshaped the cultural imagination. It gave rise to the environmental movement, inspired new sciences of systems and ecology, and embedded the idea that the planet itself is alive into the culture.

In the decades that followed, technology expanded the reach of our seeing. Satellite imagery made the whole Earth continuously observable: forests shrinking, glaciers collapsing, storms forming in real time. Microscopy revealed networks of neurons firing, cells dividing, viruses mutating. Telescopes peered to the edges of the universe, returning images of galaxies colliding and stars being born. Today, cameras can show bacteria and galaxies with equal fidelity.

Each age has found its own surface, its own technique, but the effort has always been an attempt at understanding both the lives of others and our own experience. Across this long arc, from the walls of Chauvet to the cyanotypes of Atkins, from Muybridge’s horses to Brand’s Whole Earth, images of the living world have not only recorded what is here, but affected how we think and feel about it. They remind us that seeing is never neutral: It is always an act of framing, and an invitation to imagine our place within the larger weave of existence.

The frame itself is an extraordinary tool of delineation. As an editor, it decides what belongs and what does not. The power of the frame lies in its capacity to determine which information matters.

My focus then started to come out in how I made pictures. The subject itself mattered less to me than resolving how to present it. Eventually, I found the pure white background, where the image becomes an encounter: a relation between two living beings shaped by a shared evolutionary history.

The white background was never an aesthetic choice. It’s purely functional. We never look at something singularly, but rather are simultaneously looking at the subject in relation to ourselves. I’ve often felt that our sense of ourselves is not a figure, it’s an abstraction. We don’t think of ourselves as a figure in relation to the ground. And so when wanting to provide the opportunity of connection to the subject—the other—that subject presented on white creates a shared plane for it and us. White space isn’t empty. It’s a condition that allows perception to happen. It lets the image reach the body before it’s interpreted by the mind. In that space, assumptions can fall away and the subject can be encountered in a preconceptual field, before meaning has settled.

2026 Popular Science Home of the Future Awards

39 products that will improve your everyday life.

Artist and creative director Andrew Zuckerman has spent 30 years traveling the world capturing the alluring and spectacular world of nature. Known for creating hyper-real images set against stark backgrounds, his artworks have been exhibited and acquired by public institutions and private collections throughout the world, and his books have been published in several international editions. Alongside his artistic practice, he has advised and led creative work for pioneering companies in technology, climate innovation, and energy, including Apple and Google. He lives in New York City with his wife and three children.

What Couples Married For 40 Years Do When Life Gets Hard

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Couples married for decades reveal what they turned to when they thought their marriage might end.

Couples married for decades reveal what they turned to when they thought their marriage might end.

When people in decades-long marriages describe what helped them through a crisis, most of the responses they give involve leaning on each other.

They describe listening to each other, talking through painful subjects and sometimes having the same argument more than once.

The researchers suggest that this response grows out of years of knowing they can count on their partner.

In attachment theory, a partner can act as a safe haven, someone whose presence makes it easier to bear distress.

Getting through a difficult period together may deepen that trust, making it easier to ask for help the next time it is needed.

Dr Christian Heim, the study’s first author, and Dr Caroline Heim, study co-author, interviewed each couple together, asking each person to describe what had put their marriage under strain and what had helped them cope.

The 180 participants were born in 24 countries and, with a few exceptions, had been married for at least 40 years.

Around two-thirds of the couples described a threat they believed could have ended their marriage, such as the death or serious illness of a child, infidelity or prolonged periods apart.

Talking, listening and arguing accounted for almost a quarter of the coping responses the researchers recorded.

After an affair, some couples described arguing over it repeatedly, while bereaved partners sometimes waited for a moment when both felt able to speak.

One partner in an American couple married for 45 years recalled asking their spouse about the son they had lost.

“Can I talk about our son? I’m hurting. Is it okay to talk about it?”

Some partners described taking time to grieve separately before they were ready to turn to each other.

Around four in five coping responses involved leaning on the relationship, whether by drawing closer, persevering as a couple or giving up something one partner wanted.

The interviews capture what couples in lasting marriages felt had helped them, leaving open whether the same approaches would have helped couples who separated.

When partners see a problem as something they face together, they may spend less time working out who is to blame and more time trying to understand what the other person is going through.

A previous analysis involving almost 18,000 people found that coping together and helping a partner manage stress were both linked to greater relationship satisfaction (Falconier et al., 2015).

Some couples in the interviews felt that an illness, a bereavement or a family conflict had left them closer, even though the experience itself had been painful.

A British couple married for 60 years described how they got through a serious conflict with their in-laws:

The study was published in the journal The American Journal of Family Therapy (Heim & Heim, 2025).

Psychologist, Jeremy Dean, PhD is the founder and author of PsyBlog. He holds a doctorate in psychology from University College London and two other advanced degrees in psychology. He has been writing about scientific research on PsyBlog since 2004. View all posts by Dr Jeremy Dean

Muniz rescues late point for Fulham against Newcastle after Murphy stunner

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Questions over whether Álvaro Arbeloa and Matthias Jaissle have what it takes to succeed in the Premier League remain unanswered after these two young foreign managers watched their sides grind out a 1-1 draw at Craven Cottage.

Arbeloa’s Fulham lacked fizz, physicality and personality before rescuing a point thanks to a late equaliser from Rodrigo Muniz, while Newcastle displayed little of Jaissle’s high-octane philosophy and squandered the chance to move into the top half after failing to build on a vicious opening goal from Jacob Murphy.

A second consecutive away win in the league was on the cards when Murphy seized on an error from César Palacios and scored in the 80th minute. Jaissle has faced criticism over his tactics and training methods since replacing Eddie Howe just before the start of the season but the German was happy with elements of this Newcastle performance.

The 38-year-old saw “a complete commitment and togetherness in all phases of the game” and was entitled to argue the visitors had the better chances, an impressive feat given there was such little threat from Jaissle’s front two of William Osula and Yoane Wissa.

It remains a big problem for Newcastle, who are paying for their failure to fill the void left by Alexander Isak’s move to Liverpool last year. They witnessed another half-hearted display from Wissa before the £55m striker’s withdrawal in the 61st minute and were hindered by the bluntness of Osula, whose hopes of grabbing a rare goal were somewhat hindered by his failure to register a touch in Fulham’s area.

To make matters worse Osula was also partially responsible for the leveller. Newcastle needed the 23-year-old to hold the ball up but he was outmuscled near the halfway line, allowing Fulham to pounce. Palacios, who had given the ball away for Murphy’s goal, wriggled into the area and combined with another of Arbeloa’s substitutes by smuggling the ball across for Muniz to score his first league goal since August 2025.

Jaissle felt it was a “slapstick” concession. Arbeloa, who likes to play an expansive style of passing football, focused more on Muniz’s impact after the target man’s introduction in place of the smaller Gonzalo García. “Newcastle were marking us man to man,” Fulham’s Spanish head coach said. “They forced us to play long balls and Rodrigo is very strong in this situation. He got his chance. It is very important for us because I need a player like Rodrigo. He can score goals but create so many chances.”

It is encouraging that Arbeloa was willing to be flexible and more direct. However this was a poor game between two sides too close to the bottom three for comfort. Fulham also fretted over Josh King after the 19-year-old went off with a groin injury. The attacking midfielder is set for a scan and will hope not to have suffered any significant damage after earning his first England call-up on Friday.

Thomas Tuchel will be keen to have King available. Newcastle paid a lot of attention to the teenager, with Sean Steur and Nico González sitting deep in midfield, but they could not keep him quiet during the first half. King tested Lukas Hornicek with a volley from 20 yards and later missed a decent headed chance.

Fulham were not at their best. Oscar Bobb and García went close after an error from Steur but Newcastle were comfortable. Jaissle, who made four changes after accusing his players of not following instructions during last Monday’s defeat by Everton, asked his side to play a patient game and was annoyed when they had a goal ruled out in the 33rd minute. Dan Burn did nothing wrong when he converted a cross from close range, but Wissa was penalised for a holding offence on Timothy Castagne off the ball.

Kick off your evenings with the Guardian's take on the world of football

Jaissle called it a soft decision. Newcastle, who saw Bernd Leno twice thwart Harvey Barnes before half-time, moved on and improved in the second half. They pressed with more intensity and created opportunities for Barnes and Wissa.

Fulham had no out ball with García unable to impose himself against Burn and Malick Thiaw. Palacios came on and was at fault when Newcastle broke the deadlock. The Spaniard played an aimless pass across his own half and was punished by Murphy, who seized possession before driving forward to cut inside and crack a brilliant shot past Leno from 25 yards.

It seemed Newcastle were about to ease the pressure on Jaissle. Fulham had other ideas. Arbeloa praised the defiance. Palacios linked with Alex Iwobi and found Muniz. The ball bounced around the six-yard box and Muniz forced it past Hornicek.

Fulham poured forward in search of a winner. Lewis Hall almost restored Newcastle’s lead, only for Leno to deny the left-back. A draw was fair.
